From Proposition
3.6.8
, we deduce the following Lemma.

Lemma 3.6.9
.

For any
S,S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}
such that
S,S^{\prime}\subseteq T
, we have an equality

\mu_{A(\mathcal{B},\sigma)}(\Gamma_{S},\Gamma_{S^{\prime}})=\mu(S,S^{\prime})

(3.6.6)

Consider now an admissible graph
\Gamma\in A(\mathcal{B},\sigma)
and the admissible
F
-stable torus
S_{\Gamma}
associated to
\Gamma
. We have the following proposition.

Proposition 3.6.10
.

With the notations introduced in
section

3.6.2
, we have

[S_{\Gamma}]=\omega_{\Gamma}.

Fix now
\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma\in A(\mathcal{B},\sigma)
such that
\Gamma\leq\Gamma^{\prime}
, and denote by
S=S_{\Gamma}
and
S^{\prime}=S_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
. We use the notations introduced before Lemma
3.6.4
. We have
S\subseteq S^{\prime}
. Assume that the torus
S
is
\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
conjugated to
\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{r}(Z_{\beta_{j}})_{d_{j}}
.

The permutation
\sigma^{d_{j}}:\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma}_{j,1}\to\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma}_{j,1}
determines an associated
F
-stable subtorus
T_{j}\subseteq\Gl_{\beta_{j}}
, as explained in
section

3.2.1
,
section

3.2.2
. The admissible graphs
\Gamma^{\prime}_{j,1}
correspond to admissible tori
S^{\prime}_{j}\subseteq T_{j}\subseteq\Gl_{\beta_{j}}
, for each
j=1,\dots,r
.

From Lemma
3.6.9
and Lemma
3.6.4
, we deduce the following Proposition

Proposition 3.6.11
.

We have an equality

\displaystyle\mu(S,S^{\prime})=\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mu(Z_{\beta_{j}},S^{\prime}_{j})=\prod_{j=1}^{r}C^{o}_{[S^{\prime}_{j}]},

(3.6.7)

where the last equality is a consequence of Proposition
3.6.3
.

Example 3.6.12
.

Consider the set

is the maximal torus of diagonal matrices. More precisely, we have that

.

With the notations just introduced, we have
\mathcal{B}=\{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4},\epsilon_{5}\}
,
\sigma
is the identity and

\mathcal{B}_{1}=\{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\}\ \ \ \mathcal{B}_{2}=\{\epsilon_{3}\}

and

\mathcal{B}_{3}=\{\epsilon_{4}\}\ \ \ \mathcal{B}_{4}=\{\epsilon_{5}\}.

The graph
\Gamma_{S_{1}}
associated to the torus
S_{1}
is
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{2}}

The graph

is

is
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{2}}

Notice that
\Gamma_{S_{1}},\Gamma_{S_{2}}\leq\Gamma_{S_{3}}
and we have corresponding inclusions
S_{1},S_{2}\subseteq S_{3}

Example 3.6.13
.

Consider
I=\{1,2\}
and the dimension vector

, i.e.

and let

be the torus

where

is the torus of Example

. In this case, we have
\mathcal{B}=\{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4}\}
with

\mathcal{B}_{1}=\{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\}\ \ \ \mathcal{B}_{2}=\{\epsilon_{3},\epsilon_{4}\},

and
\sigma
is the permutation
\sigma=(12)(34)\in S_{4}
.

Let
S\subseteq\Gl_{\beta}
be the admissible subtorus

S=\Biggl\{\left(\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\lambda\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\mu&0\\
0&\mu\end{pmatrix}\right)\ |\ \lambda,\mu\in\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}}^{*}\Biggr\}.

We have that
S\subseteq T
and the graph
\Gamma_{S}
is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{4}}

Notice that
\Gamma_{S}
has two connected components which are both stabilized by

. With the notations introduced before, we have therefore two orbits
O_{1},O_{2}
with
\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma_{S}}_{1}=\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma_{S}}_{2}=\mathcal{B}_{2}
and
d_{1}=d_{2}=1
. Denote by
\Gamma_{S,1},\Gamma_{S,2}
the restriction of

to
\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}
respectively.

Notice moreover that the associated elements
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
are given by

\beta_{1}=(2,0)\ \ \ \beta_{2}=(0,2)

and, from Proposition
3.6.10
we have that

[S]=\omega_{\beta_{1}}\ast\omega_{\beta_{2}}.

The torus
S
is indeed
Z_{\beta_{1}}\times Z_{\beta_{2}}
embedded block diagonally in
\Gl_{\alpha}
.

Moreover, from Proposition
3.6.11
, we deduce that

\mu(S,T)=\mu_{\mathcal{B}_{1},\sigma}(\Gamma_{S,1},\Gamma_{T_{\epsilon}})\mu_{\mathcal{B}_{2},\sigma}(\Gamma_{S,2},\Gamma_{T_{\epsilon}})=\mu(Z_{2},T_{\epsilon})^{2},

(3.6.8)

where
Z_{2}=Z_{\Gl_{2}}
.

Eq.(
3.6.8
) can be checked directly from the definition of the Möbius function
\mu
. We have

\{Z_{\beta_{1}}\times Z_{\beta_{2}},Z_{\beta_{1}}\times T_{\epsilon},T_{\epsilon}\times Z_{\beta_{2}}\}=\{S^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}\ |\ Z_{\beta_{1}}\times Z_{\beta_{2}}\leq S^{\prime\prime}\lneqq T_{\epsilon}\times T_{\epsilon}\}.

From. eq(
3.5.5
), we deduce that we have

\mu(Z_{\beta_{1}}\times Z_{\beta_{2}},Z_{\beta_{1}}\times T_{\epsilon})=\mu(Z_{\beta_{1}}\times Z_{\beta_{2}},T_{\epsilon}\times Z_{\beta_{2}})=\mu(Z_{2},T_{\epsilon})=-1

and thus from eq.(
3.5.5
) that we have

\mu(S,T_{\epsilon}\times T_{\epsilon})=-(-1-1+1)=1=\mu(Z_{2},T_{\epsilon})^{2}.

Consider now the admissible and
\sigma
-stable graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
given by
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{4}}

and denote by
S^{\prime}=S_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
. The torus
S^{\prime}
is given by

S^{\prime}=\Biggl\{\left(\dfrac{1}{x^{q}-x}\begin{pmatrix}ax^{q}-bx&-a+b\\
(a-b)xx^{q}&-ax+bx^{q}\end{pmatrix},\dfrac{1}{x^{q}-x}\begin{pmatrix}ax^{q}-bx&-a+b\\
(a-b)xx^{q}&-ax+bx^{q}\end{pmatrix}\right)\ |\ a,b\in\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}}^{*}\Biggr\}.

(3.6.9)

In this case, the graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
has
2
connected components, which are swapped by
\sigma
. We have therefore a single orbit
O_{1}
of length
d_{1}=2
with

\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{1,1}=\{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{3}\}\ \ \ \mathcal{B}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{1,2}=\{\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{4}\}.

Notice that the associated dimension vector
\beta_{1}^{\prime}
is
\beta_{1}^{\prime}=(1,1)
.
Proposition
3.6.10
states therefore that
S^{\prime}
is
\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugated to the torus

(Z_{(1,1)})_{2}\subseteq\Gl_{\alpha},

which is also directly seen by the expression of
S^{\prime}
in eq.(
3.6.9
).

Notice that

\sigma^{2}=\mathrm{Id}:\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{1,1}\to\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{1,1}.

From Lemma
3.6.9
and Lemma
3.6.4
, we find therefore that

\mu(S^{\prime},T)=\mu_{\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{1,1},\mathrm{Id}}(\Gamma^{\prime}_{1,1},\Gamma_{T_{2}})=\mu(Z_{(1,1)},Z_{(1,0)}\times Z_{(0,1)}).

3.7
Inclusion of admissible subtori

Let
\nu,\nu^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}^{ss}_{\alpha}
. Fix a maximal torus
T
such that
S_{\nu^{\prime}}\subseteq T\subseteq\Gl_{\alpha}.
Define the set
P_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
as

P_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}\coloneqq\{S\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}\ \ |\ [S]=\nu\ ,S\leq S_{\nu^{\prime}}\}.

In this paragraph we give a combinatorial description of
P_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
which will be used in the proof of Theorem
4.1.2
.

Assume that

\nu=\psi_{d_{1}}(\omega_{\beta_{1}})\ast\cdots\ast\psi_{d_{r}}(\omega_{\beta_{r}})

and

\nu^{\prime}=\psi_{d^{\prime}_{1}}(\omega_{\beta^{\prime}_{1}})\ast\cdots\ast\psi_{d_{t}^{\prime}}(\omega_{\beta^{\prime}_{t}})

respectively.

Up to reordering the factors in the product
\nu=\prod_{j=1}^{r}\psi_{d_{j}}(\omega_{\beta_{j}})
, we can assume that there exists a strictly increasing sequence
i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\in\{1,\dots,r\}
such that:

•

(d_{j},\beta_{j})=(d_{1},\beta_{1})
for
j=1,\dots,i_{1}

•

(d_{j},\beta_{j})=(d_{i_{p}},\beta_{i_{p}})
for all
i_{p-1}<j\leq i_{p}
for
p\in\{2,\dots,k\}

•

(d_{i_{p}},\beta_{i_{p}})\neq(d_{i_{s}},\beta_{i_{s}})
for any
p\neq s
.

We have that
i_{1}=\nu((d_{1},\beta_{1}))
and, for each
h\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, we have that
\displaystyle i_{h}-i_{h-1}=\nu((d_{i_{h}},\beta_{i_{h}}))
. Let
M_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
be the set of partitions of
\{1,\dots,t\}
into
r
non-empty disjoint subsets
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
with the following properties:

•

If
h
belongs to
X_{i}
, then
d_{i}|d^{\prime}_{h}

•

For every
j=1,\dots,r
, it holds
\displaystyle\sum_{h\in X_{j}}\dfrac{d^{\prime}_{h}}{d_{j}}\beta^{\prime}_{h}=\beta_{j}
.

We will denote the element of
M_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
associated to the subsets
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
by
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})
.

Consider now the group
W^{\prime}_{\nu}
defined as

W^{\prime}_{\nu}\coloneqq S_{\nu((d_{i_{1}},\beta_{i_{1}}))}\times\cdots\times S_{\nu((d_{i_{k}},\beta_{i_{k}}))}.

The set
M_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
is endowed with an action of the group
W^{\prime}_{\nu}

defined as follows. Consider an element
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})\in W^{\prime}_{\nu}
and an element
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})\in M_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
. We put

\sigma\cdot(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})\coloneqq(X_{\sigma_{1}(1)},\dots,X_{\sigma_{1}(i_{1})},X_{\sigma_{2}(i_{1}+1)},\dots,X_{\sigma_{k}(r)}).

The group
W_{\nu}^{\prime}
acts freely on
M_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
. We denote by
\overline{M_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}}
the quotient set
M_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}/W^{\prime}_{\nu}
. We will now define the following morphism

\pi_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}:P_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}\to\overline{M_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}}.

We denote
by
\Gamma^{\prime}
the graph associated to
S_{\nu^{\prime}}
with respect to the torus
T
. Let
\{\mathcal{B}_{j,h}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1,\dots,t\\
h=1,\dots,d_{j}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}
be the partition of the set
\mathcal{B}
introduced in paragraph
section

3.6
for the graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Consider an
F
-stable admissible torus
S\subseteq S_{\nu^{\prime}}
with
[S]=\nu
and the corresponding
\sigma
-stable graph
\Gamma\leq\Gamma^{\prime}
, i.e.
\Omega_{\Gamma}\supseteq\Omega_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
.

Let
O_{1},\dots,O_{r}
be the
r
orbits for the action of
\sigma
on the connected components of
\Gamma
of length
d_{1},\dots,d_{r}
respectively and assume to have fixed representatives
\Gamma_{1,1},\dots,\Gamma_{r,1}
for each of the orbits.

For each
j=1,\dots,r
, there exists a subset
X_{j}\subseteq\{1,\dots,t\}
and, for each
l\in X_{j}
, a subset
Z_{l}\subseteq\{1,\dots,d^{\prime}_{l}\}
, such that
\Gamma_{j,1}
is the complete graph with vertices

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{l\in X_{j}}\bigsqcup_{z\in Z_{l}}\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{l,z}.

The subsets
X_{j}
do not depend on the choice of the representatives
\Gamma_{1,1},\dots,\Gamma_{r,1}
and form a partition of the set
\{1,\dots,t\}
. The partition
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})
belongs to
M_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
.

Indeed, since the orbit
O_{j}
has length
d_{j}
, we must have that

\sigma^{s}\left(\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{l\in X_{j}}\bigsqcup_{z\in Z_{l}}\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{l,z}\right)\cap\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{l\in X_{j}}\bigsqcup_{z\in Z_{l}}\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{l,z}=\varnothing

for any
0<s\leq d_{j}-1
and

\sigma^{d_{j}}\left(\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{l\in X_{j}}\bigsqcup_{z\in Z_{l}}\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{l,z}\right)=\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{l\in X_{j}}\bigsqcup_{z\in Z_{l}}\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{l,z}.

Recall that
\sigma(\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{l,z})=\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{l,z+1}
, where the index
z
of
\mathcal{B}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{l,z}
is considered modulo
d_{l}^{\prime}
. We deduce therefore that
Z_{l}
is such that

(Z_{l}+s)\cap Z_{s}=\varnothing\mod d_{j}

for each
0<s\leq d_{j}-1
and

Z_{l}+d_{j}=Z_{j}\mod d_{j}.

This implies that
d_{j}|d_{l}^{\prime}
and that there exists
a_{l}\in Z_{l}
such that

Z_{l}=\left\{a_{l}+d_{j}k\ |\ k=1,\dots,\dfrac{d_{l}^{\prime}}{d_{j}}\right\}.

In particular, it holds that
|Z_{l}|=\dfrac{d_{l}^{\prime}}{d_{j}}
, from which we deduce that

\sum_{l\in X_{j}}\dfrac{d_{l}^{\prime}}{d_{j}}\beta^{\prime}_{l}=\beta_{j}.

We define then

\pi_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}(S)\coloneqq[(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})]

where
[(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})]
is the class of the element
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})
in the quotient
\overline{M_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}}}
.

The morphism
\pi_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}
is well-defined, i.e. does not depend on the choice of an ordering of the orbits
O_{1},\dots,O_{r}
and it is surjective as we are taking the class
[(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})]
in the quotient
\overline{M_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}}
for the action of
W_{\nu}^{\prime}
.

From the description of the subsets
Z_{l}
given above, we deduce that, for each
[(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})]
, the fiber
\pi_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}^{-1}([(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})])
has cardinality

|\pi^{-1}_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}([(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})])|=\prod_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}^{|X_{j}|-1}.

(3.7.1)

4
Log compatible functions and plethystic identities

In this Section, we recall the Definition of a Log compatible family, first introduced by Letellier
[
41
]
and we prove our main Theorem
4.1.2
about these families, which will be the key tool to compute the E-series of non-generic character stacks.

We first introduce the following notation.

Consider a family of rational functions indexed by multypes
\{F_{\omega}(t)\}_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{I}}\subseteq{\mathbb{Q}}(t)
. For any
V\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{I}
, we define the rational function
F_{\alpha,V}(t)\in{\mathbb{Q}}(t)
in the following way:

F_{\alpha,V}(t)\coloneqq\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)}{w(\omega)}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\leq S_{\omega}\\
S^{\prime}\text{ of level }V\end{subarray}}P_{[S^{\prime}]}(t)\mu(S^{\prime},S_{\omega})\right).

(4.0.1)

For
V=\{\alpha\}
we will use the notation
F_{\alpha,gen}(t)\coloneqq F_{\alpha,\{\alpha\}}(t)
. From Proposition
3.6.3
, we have an equality

F_{\alpha,gen}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)}{w(\omega)}(t-1)\mu(Z_{\alpha},S_{\omega})=\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)}{w(\omega)}(t-1)C^{o}_{\omega^{ss}}.

4.1
Plethysm and log compatibility: main result

We give the following definition of a Log compatible family
\{F_{\omega}(t)\}_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{I}}
.

Definition 4.1.1
.

We say that
\{F_{\omega}(t)\}_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{I}}
is
Log compatible
if for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
,
\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
and for every multitypes
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{r}
and integers
d_{1},\dots,d_{r}
such that
\psi_{d_{1}}(\nu_{1})\ast\cdots\ast\psi_{d_{r}}(\nu_{r})=\omega
, we have

\prod_{j=1}^{r}F_{\nu_{j}}(t^{d_{j}})=F_{\omega}(t).

We have the following theorem:

Theorem 4.1.2
.

For a Log compatible family
\{F_{\omega}(t)\}_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{I}}
and any
V\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{I}
, we have the following equality:

\Plexp\left(\sum_{\beta\in V}F_{\beta,gen}(t)y^{\beta}\right)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}F_{\alpha,V}(t)y^{\alpha}.

(4.1.1)

Remark 4.1.3
.

The notion of a Log compatible family and the definition of the polynomials
F_{\alpha,gen}(t)
had already been introduced in
[
41
, Paragraph 2.1.2]

Letellier
[
41
, Theorem 2.2]
used these objects to show the case in which
V=\mathbb{N}^{I}
of Theorem
4.1.2
. However, Letellier’s proof does not seem to extend immediately to the case of any level
V
.

Notice that, for
V=\mathbb{N}^{I}
, we have that
\displaystyle F_{\alpha,\mathbb{N}^{I}}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)}{w(\omega)}|(S_{\omega}^{reg})^{F}|
.

To obtain the equality

\Plexp\left(\sum_{\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}F_{\beta,gen}(t)y^{\beta}\right)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)}{w(\omega)}|(S_{\omega}^{reg})^{F}|,

the author
[
41
]
uses in a key way two classical lemmas regarding plethystic operations, i.e.
[
41
, Lemma 2.1]
and
[
51
, Lemma 22]
.

These two latter statements do not have an analog when we truncate the sum inside the plethystic exponential
\displaystyle\Plexp\left(\sum_{\beta\in V}F_{\beta,gen}(t)y^{\beta}\right)
at a general
V
. For this reason, we needed to study the combinatorial objects introduced in
section

2
and
section

3
.

In particular, in the proof, we will need the following three main tools.

•

We will use the ring of multitypes introduced in
section

2.3.1
and in particular Lemma
2.3.4
to better understand the truncated exponentials
\displaystyle\Plexp\left(\sum_{\beta\in V}F_{\beta,gen}(t)y^{\beta}\right)
.

•

We use the results of
section

3.6.2
and in particular Proposition
3.6.11
to relate the terms of the form
C^{o}_{\omega_{1}}\cdots C^{o}_{\omega_{r}}
appearing in the LHS of eq.(
4.1.1
) to the terms of the form
\mu(S,S_{\omega})
appearing in the RHS of eq.(
4.1.1
).

•

We use the results of
section

3.7
to relate combinatorially the sums of the type

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\leq S_{\omega}\\
S^{\prime}\text{ of level }V\end{subarray}}\mu(S^{\prime},S_{\omega})P_{[S^{\prime}]}(t)

appearing in the RHS of eq.(
4.1.1
) to the LHS of eq.(
4.1.1
).

Proof of Theorem 4.1.2.

We are going to give the proof through multiple steps.

I Step: rewriting the LHS of eq.(
4.1.1
)
.

We start by using the results of
section

2.3
concerning the ring of multitypes. In particular, we remark that there is a unique morphism
\Theta
of
\lambda
-rings

\Theta:\mathcal{K}^{ss}_{I}\to{\mathbb{Q}}(t)

obtained by extending

\Theta(\omega_{\alpha})=F_{\alpha,gen}(t).

By Lemma
2.3.4
, Identity (
4.1.1
) is thus equivalent to the following Identity

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathbb{T}^{ss}_{\alpha}\\
\text{level }V\end{subarray}}\dfrac{\Theta(\nu)}{w(\nu)}=F_{\alpha,V}(t)

(4.1.2)

The RHS of eq.(
4.1.2
) is given by

F_{\alpha,V}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)}{w(\omega)}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq S_{\omega}\\
\text{ level }V\end{subarray}}P_{[S]}(t)\mu(S,S_{\omega})\right).

(4.1.3)

We are left to understand the LHS of eq.(
4.1.2
).

II Step: understanding the LHS of eq.(
4.1.2
)
.

Consider now
\nu\in\mathbb{T}^{ss}_{I}
and
d_{1},\dots,d_{r}\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
and
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{r}\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
such that

\nu=\psi_{d_{1}}(\omega_{\beta_{1}})\ast\cdots\ast\psi_{d_{r}}(\omega_{\beta_{r}}).

The value of
\dfrac{\Theta(\nu)}{w(\nu)}
is given by

\dfrac{1}{w(\nu)}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\psi_{d_{j}}(\Theta(\beta_{j}))=\dfrac{1}{w(\nu)}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\left(\sum_{\omega_{j}\in\mathbb{T}_{\beta_{j}}}\dfrac{F_{\omega_{j}}(t^{d_{j}})}{w(\omega_{j})}(t^{d_{j}}-1)\mu(Z_{\beta_{j}},S_{\omega_{j}})\right)=

(4.1.4)

\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)P_{\nu}(t)}{w(\nu)}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{\beta_{1}},\dots,\omega_{r}\in\mathbb{T}_{\beta_{r}}\\
\psi_{d_{1}}(\omega_{1})\ast\cdots\ast\psi_{d_{r}}(\omega_{r})=\omega\end{subarray}}\dfrac{1}{w(\omega_{1})\cdots w(\omega_{r})}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mu(Z_{\beta_{j}},S_{\omega_{j}})\right)

(4.1.5)

where the equality of eq.(
4.1.4
) and eq.(
4.1.5
) is a direct consequence of the Log compatibility of the family
\{F_{\omega}(t)\}_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{I}}
.

III Step: the function
f_{\nu,\omega}
.

Fix a multitype
\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
with
\omega=(d^{\prime}_{1},\bm{\lambda}_{1})\cdots(d_{t}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda}_{t})
for multipartitions
\bm{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\bm{\lambda}_{t}

and its associated admissible torus
S_{\omega}\subseteq\Gl_{\alpha}
.

Denote by
H_{\nu,\omega}
the set defined by

H_{\nu,\omega}\coloneqq\{(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})\in\mathbb{T}_{\beta_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{T}_{\beta_{r}}\ |\ \psi_{d_{1}}(\omega_{1})\ast\cdots\ast\psi_{d_{r}}(\omega_{r})=\omega\}

and by
\delta_{\nu}:H_{\nu,\omega}\to\mathbb{Z}
the function defined as

\delta_{\nu}((\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r}))\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mu(Z_{\beta_{j}},S_{\omega_{j}}).

Let
M_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}
be the set introduced in
section

3.7
. Consider the following function

f_{\nu,\omega}:M_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}\to H_{\nu,\omega}

defined as:

f_{\nu,\omega}((X_{1},\dots,X_{r}))=(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})

where

\omega_{i}(d,\bm{\lambda})=\#\{h\in X_{i}\text{ such that }\left(\dfrac{d^{\prime}_{h}}{d_{i}},\bm{\lambda}_{h}\right)=(d,\bm{\lambda})\}

for every
(d,\bm{\lambda})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathcal{P}^{I}
.

We will now show that the function
f_{\nu,\omega}
is surjective and for each
(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})
, the cardinality of the fiber is given by

|f^{-1}_{\nu,\omega}(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})|=\prod_{(d,\bm{\lambda})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathcal{P}^{I}}\dfrac{\omega(d,\bm{\lambda})!}{\omega_{1}(d,\bm{\lambda})!\cdots\omega_{r}(d,\bm{\lambda})!}

(4.1.6)

As done in
section

3.7
for the semisimple multitype
\nu
, for the multitype
\omega=(d_{1}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda}_{1}^{\prime})\dots(d_{t}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda}_{t}^{\prime})
there exists a a strictly increasing sequence
j_{1}<\dots<j_{c}\in\{1,\dots,t\}
such that:

•

(d^{\prime}_{j},\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}_{j})=(d^{\prime}_{1},\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}_{1})
for
j=1,\dots,j_{1}
.

•

(d^{\prime}_{j},\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}_{j})=(d^{\prime}_{j_{p}},\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}_{j_{p}})
for all
j_{p-1}<j\leq j_{p}
for
p\in\{2,\dots,c\}
.

•

(d^{\prime}_{j_{p}},\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}_{j_{p}})\neq(d^{\prime}_{j_{s}},\bm{\lambda}_{j_{s}}^{\prime})
if
p\neq s
.

Recall that
j_{p}-j_{p-1}=\omega((d_{j_{p}}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda}_{j_{p}}^{\prime}))
for every
1\leq p\leq c
, where we put
j_{0}=0
. To prove the surjectivity of
f_{\nu,\omega}
, fix
(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})\in H_{\nu,\omega}
and define subsets
X_{\omega_{1}},\dots,X_{\omega_{r}}\subseteq\{1,\dots,t\}
as follows.

X_{\omega_{j}}=\bigsqcup_{l=1}^{c}\{h\in\{j_{l-1},\dots,j_{l}\}\ |\ \sum_{m=1}^{j-1}\psi_{d_{m}}(\omega_{m})((d_{j_{l}}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda}_{j_{l}}^{\prime}))<h-j_{l-1}\leq\sum_{m=1}^{j}\psi_{d_{m}}(\omega_{m})((d_{j_{l}}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda}_{j_{l}}^{\prime}))\}.

We have that
(X_{\omega_{1}},\dots,X_{\omega_{r}})\in H_{\nu,\omega}
and
f_{\nu,\omega}((X_{\omega_{1}},\dots,X_{\omega_{r}}))=(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})
.

Through a similar argument, we see that the cardinality of
|f^{-1}_{\nu,\omega}(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})|
is the product, for
l=1,\dots,c
of the number of partitions of a set of cardinality
\omega((d_{j_{l}}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda}_{j_{l}}^{\prime}))=j_{l}-j_{l-1}
into
r
sets of cardinality
\psi_{d_{1}}(\omega_{1})((d_{j_{l}}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda}_{j_{l}}^{\prime})),\dots,\psi_{d_{r}}(\omega_{r})((d_{j_{l}}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda}_{j_{l}}^{\prime}))
respectively, i.e.

|f^{-1}_{\nu,\omega}(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})|=\prod_{l=1}^{c}\dfrac{\omega((d_{j_{l}}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda}_{j_{l}}^{\prime}))!}{(\psi_{d_{1}}(\omega_{1})((d_{j_{l}}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda}_{j_{l}}^{\prime})))!\cdots(\psi_{d_{r}}(\omega_{r})((d_{j_{l}}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda}_{j_{l}}^{\prime})))!}=

(4.1.7)

=\prod_{(d,\bm{\lambda})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathcal{P}^{I}}\dfrac{\omega(d,\bm{\lambda})!}{\omega_{1}(d,\bm{\lambda})!\cdots\omega_{r}(d,\bm{\lambda})!}.

(4.1.8)

IV Step: describe the summands inside the parenthesis of eq.(
4.1.5
) for any
\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
.

For any
(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})\in H_{\nu,\omega}
, we have the following equality:

\prod_{(d,\bm{\lambda})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathcal{P}^{I}}\dfrac{\omega(d,\bm{\lambda})!}{\omega_{1}(d,\bm{\lambda})!\cdots\omega_{r}(d,\bm{\lambda})!}=\dfrac{w(\omega)}{w(\omega_{1})\cdots w(\omega_{r})}\dfrac{\prod_{j=1}^{r}\prod_{h\in X_{j}}\left(\frac{d^{\prime}_{h}}{d_{j}}\right)}{\prod_{l=1}^{t}d^{\prime}_{l}}.

(4.1.9)

As
\displaystyle\bigsqcup X_{j}=\{1,\dots,t\}
, the right hand side of eq.(
4.1.9
) is equal to

\dfrac{w(\omega)}{w(\omega_{1})\cdots w(\omega_{r})}\dfrac{1}{\prod_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}^{|X_{j}|}}.

(4.1.10)

For an element
m=(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})\in M_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}
, denote by
d_{m}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}^{|X_{j}|}
.

For each
\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
, we can thus rewrite the corresponding summand appearing in the RHS of eq.(
4.1.5
) as:

\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)P_{\nu}(t)}{w(\nu)}\left(\sum_{(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})\in H_{\nu,\omega}}\dfrac{1}{w(\omega_{1})\cdots w(\omega_{r})}\delta_{\nu}((\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r}))\right)=

(4.1.11)

\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)P_{\nu}(t)}{w(\nu)w(\omega)}\left(\sum_{m\in M_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}}\dfrac{w(\omega)}{w(\omega_{1})\cdots w(\omega_{r})}\dfrac{\delta_{\nu}(f_{\nu,\omega}(m))}{|f_{\nu,\omega}^{-1}(m)|}\right)=\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)P_{\nu}(t)}{w(\nu)w(\omega)}\left(\sum_{m\in M_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}}\delta_{\nu}(f_{\nu,\omega}(m))d_{m}\right),

(4.1.12)

where the last equality is a consequence of eq.(
4.1.7
), eq(
4.1.9
),eq(
4.1.10
).

V Step: use the results of
section

3.7
to rewrite the RHS of eq.(
4.1.12
)

The set
H_{\nu,\omega}
is endowed with the following action of
W^{\prime}_{\nu}
. We use the notations of
section

3.7
. An element
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})\in W^{\prime}_{\nu}
acts on
(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})\in H_{\nu,\omega}
by

\sigma\cdot(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r})=(\omega_{\sigma_{1}(1)},\dots,\omega_{\sigma_{1}(i_{1})},\omega_{\sigma_{2}(1)},\dots,\omega_{\sigma_{k}(r)}).

Notice that the function
\delta_{\nu}
is
W_{\nu}^{\prime}
invariant and
f_{\nu,\omega}
is
W_{\nu}^{\prime}
equivariant. The function

\delta_{\nu}\circ f_{\nu,\omega}:M_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}\to\mathbb{Z}

is therefore
W_{\nu}^{\prime}
invariant and descends to a function
\overline{M_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}}\to\mathbb{Z}
which we still denote by
\delta_{\nu}\circ f_{\nu,\omega}
. Notice moreover that the quantity
d_{m}
is
W^{\prime}_{\nu}
invariant too and so
d_{m}
is well defined for an element
m\in\overline{M_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}}
.

The right hand side of eq.(
4.1.11
) is therefore equal to

\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)P_{\nu}(t)}{w(\omega)}\left(\sum_{m\in\overline{M_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}}}\delta_{\nu}(f_{\nu,\omega}(m))\dfrac{d_{m}|W_{\nu}^{\prime}|}{w(\nu)}\right).

(4.1.13)

For any
m\in\overline{M}_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}
, we have

\dfrac{d_{m}|W_{\nu}^{\prime}|}{w(\nu)}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}\dfrac{d_{j}^{|X_{j}|}}{d_{j}}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}^{|X_{j}|-1}

By eq.(
3.7.1
), we can thus rewrite the sum of eq.(
4.1.13
) as

\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)P_{\nu}(t)}{w(\omega)}\left(\sum_{S\in P_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}}\delta_{\nu}(f_{\nu,\omega}(\pi_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}(S)))\right).

(4.1.14)

From Proposition
3.6.11
, we see that
\delta_{\nu}(f_{\nu,\omega}(\pi_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}(S)))=\mu(S,S_{\omega})
and so, from eq.(
4.1.11
), we deduce that
\dfrac{\Theta(\nu)}{w(\nu)}
is equal to

\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)P_{\nu}(t)}{w(\omega)}\sum_{S\in P_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}}\mu(S,S_{\omega})

(4.1.15)

Summing over the
\nu\in\mathbb{T}^{ss}_{\alpha}
of level
V
, we have therefore:

\Coeff_{\alpha}\left(\Plexp\left(\sum_{\beta\in V}F_{\beta,gen}(t)y^{\beta}\right)\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathbb{T}^{ss}_{\alpha}\\
\text{of level }V\end{subarray}}\dfrac{\Theta(\nu)}{w(\nu)}=

(4.1.16)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\in\mathbb{T}^{ss}_{\alpha}\\
\text{of level }V\end{subarray}}\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)P_{\nu}(t)}{w(\omega)}\sum_{S\in P_{\nu,\omega^{ss}}}\mu(S,S_{\omega})=\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)}{w(\omega)}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\leq S_{\omega}\\
\text{ of level }V\end{subarray}}P_{[S]}(t)\mu(S,S_{\omega})\right)

(4.1.17)

The right hand side is equal to
F_{\alpha,V}(t)
by eq.(
4.1.3
).
∎

5
Computation of convolution product for products of finite general linear groups

In this section, we will use Theorem
4.1.2
to compute some invariants of certain class functions of
\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. These results will be used to understand the cohomology of multiplicative quiver stacks and character stacks for Riemann surfaces in
section

6.5
and
section

7
.

We will start by reviewing some generalities about convolution product for the class functions of a finite group. The finite groups that will interest us will be
finite reductive groups.

In particular, consider the following situation. In this section,
G
is a reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with associated Frobenius
F:G\to G
.

The finite group
G^{F}
is called a finite reductive group. We will recall some generalities about the representation theory of the group
G^{F}
in
section

5.2
,
section

5.3
. We follow the book
[
18
]
.

We fix a prime
\ell
such that
(\ell,q)=1
and an isomorphism
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}\cong\mathbb{C}
. We will identify
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}
-vector spaces with
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces through this isomorphism in the following.

5.1
Convolution product of class functions of a finite group

Let
H
be a finite group. We denote by
\mathcal{C}(H)
the set of complex class functions, i.e. the functions
f:H\to\mathbb{C}
which are constant on the conjugacy classes of
H
. We denote by
1\in\mathcal{C}(H)
the constant function equal to
1
.

For
f,g\in\mathcal{C}(H)
, we denote by
\langle f,g\rangle
the quantity

\langle f,g\rangle=\dfrac{1}{|H|}\sum_{h\in H}f(h)\overline{g(h)}.

Recall that a basis of
\mathcal{C}(H)
is given by the irreducible characters of
H
. We will denote the set of irreducible characters of
H
by
H^{\vee}
.

The vector space
\mathcal{C}(H)
can be endowed with the following ring structure
(\mathcal{C}(H),\ast)
. Given two class functions
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(H)
, the convolution
f_{1}\ast f_{2}
is the class function defined as

f_{1}\ast f_{2}(g)=\sum_{h\in H}f_{1}(gh)f_{2}(h^{-1}).

Denote by
\Cl(H)
the set of conjugacy classes of
H
. For any
\mathcal{O}\in\Cl(H)
, we denote by
1_{\mathcal{O}}\in\mathcal{C}(H)
the characteristic function of
\mathcal{O}
. For a central element
\eta\in H
, we denote by
1_{\eta}
the characteristic function
1_{\{\eta\}}
. Notice that for any class function
f
, there is an equality

\langle f\ast 1_{\eta^{-1}},1_{e}\rangle=\dfrac{f(\eta)}{|H|}.

Recall now that

\displaystyle 1_{e}=\sum_{\chi\in H^{\vee}}\dfrac{\chi(e)}{|H|}\chi.

(5.1.1)

We have therefore

\dfrac{f(\eta)}{|H|}=\sum_{\chi\in H^{\vee}}\langle f\ast 1_{\eta^{-1}},\chi\rangle\dfrac{\chi(e)}{|H|}.

(5.1.2)

For any two class functions
f_{1},f_{2}:H\to\mathbb{C}
and an irreducible character
\chi\in H^{\vee}
, we have

\langle f_{1}\ast f_{2},\chi\rangle=\langle f_{1},\chi\rangle\langle f_{2},\chi\rangle\dfrac{|H|}{\chi(e)}

(5.1.3)

(see for example
[
34
, Theorem 2.13]
).
In particular, from eq.(
5.1.2
) and eq.(
5.1.3
), we deduce the identity:

\dfrac{f(\eta)}{|H|}=\sum_{\chi\in H^{\vee}}\langle f,\chi\rangle\langle 1_{\eta^{-1}},\chi\rangle=\sum_{\chi\in H^{\vee}}\langle f,\chi\rangle\dfrac{\chi(\eta^{-1})}{\chi(e)}\dfrac{\chi(e)}{|H|}.

(5.1.4)

5.2
Deligne-Lusztig induction

In this section, we review the definition of the Deligne-Lusztig induction for finite reductive groups. This construction, which allows to give a geometric description of the irreducible characters of finite reductive groups, was first introduced in
[
16
]
.

Consider an
F
-stable Levi subgroup
L
of
G
, a parabolic subgroup
P
having
L
as Levi factor and denote by
U_{P}
the unipotent radical of
P
. Recall that there is an isomorphism

P/U_{P}\cong L

and denote by
\pi_{L}:P\to L
the associated quotient map.

In general
P
is not
F
-stable. We can find an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup
P\supseteq L
if and only if
\epsilon_{L}=\epsilon_{G}
.

Denote by
\mathcal{L}
the Lang map
\mathcal{L}:G\to G
given by
\mathcal{L}(g)=g^{-1}F(g)
. The variety
X_{L}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}^{-1}(U_{P})
has a left
G^{F}
-action and a right
L^{F}
-action by multiplication on the left/right respectively.

This action induces an action on the compactly supported étale cohomology groups
H^{i}_{c}(X_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell})
and so endows the virtual vector space

\displaystyle H^{*}_{c}(X_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell})\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}(-1)^{i}H^{i}_{c}(X_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell})

with the structure of a virtual
G^{F}
-representation-
L^{F}
. For an
L^{F}
-representation
M
, we define the Deligne-Lusztig induction
R^{G}_{L}(M)
as the virtual
G^{F}
-representation given by

R^{G}_{L}(M)=H^{*}_{c}(X_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[L^{F}]}M.

We will denote by
R^{G}_{L}
the induced linear map

R^{G}_{L}:\mathcal{C}(L^{F})\to\mathcal{C}(G^{F}).

Remark 5.2.1
.

In the cases that will interest us in this article, i.e. when
G
is a product of factors of the type
(\Gl_{n})_{d}
, it will always be true that the functor
R^{G}_{L}
does not depend on the choice of the parabolic subgroup
P\supseteq L
(see for example
[
7
, Proposition 6.1.1]
).

Consider now a split Levi subgroup
L
, i.e. such that
\epsilon_{L}=\epsilon_{G}
. In this case, we can take an
F
-stable parabolic subgroup
P\supseteq L
. The variety
X_{L}
is a
U_{P}
-principal bundle over the finite variety
G^{F}/U_{P}^{F}
and
H_{c}^{*}(X_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell})
is therefore concentrated in degree
2\dim(U_{P})
, see the discussion before
[
18
, Lemma 9.1.5]
for more details.

If
L
is a split Levi subgroup, we have thus an equality

R^{G}_{L}(M)=\mathbb{C}[G^{F}/U_{P}^{F}]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[L^{F}]}M

for every
L^{F}
-representation
M
.

In the split case, we can give the following equivalent description of this functor. For an
L^{F}
-representation
M
, denote by
\Infl_{L^{F}}^{P^{F}}(M)
the natural lift to a
P^{F}
-representation through the quotient map
\pi_{L}
. In
[
18
, Proposition 5.18 (1)]
, it is shown the following Lemma:

Lemma 5.2.2
.

If
L\subseteq G
is a split
F
-stable Levi subgroup, we have an isomorphism of functors:

R^{G}_{L}\cong\Ind_{P^{F}}^{G^{F}}(\Infl_{L^{F}}^{P^{F}}).

Lastly, we recall the following properties of Deligne-Lusztig induction.

Lemma 5.2.3
.

For a reductive group
G
and a Levi subgroup
L\subseteq G
, we have:

1.

Given an
F
-stable Levi subgroup
L^{\prime}\supseteq L
, there is an isomorphism of functors:
R^{G}_{L^{\prime}}(R^{L^{\prime}}_{L})\cong R^{G}_{L}
.

2.

Assume there exist reductive groups
G_{1},G_{2}
and Levi subgroups
L_{1},L_{2}
such that
G=G_{1}\times G_{2}
and
L=L_{1}\times L_{2}
. For an
L_{1}^{F}
-representation
M_{1}
and an
L_{2}^{F}
-representation
M_{2}
, there is a natural isomorphism
R^{G}_{L}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})=R^{G_{1}}_{L_{1}}(M_{1})\boxtimes R^{G_{2}}_{L_{2}}(M_{2})
.

Proof.

The first point is shown in
[
18
, Proposition 9.1.8]
. For the second point, we can choose parabolic subgroups
G_{1}\supseteq P_{1}\supseteq L_{1}
and
G_{2}\supseteq P_{2}\supseteq L_{2}
such that
P_{1},P_{2}
have as Levi factor
L_{1},L_{2}
respectively. The group
P=P_{1}\times P_{2}\subseteq G
is thus a parabolic subgroup having
L
as Levi factor and
U_{P}=U_{P_{1}}\times U_{P_{2}}
.

We have therefore

\mathcal{L}^{-1}(U_{P})=\mathcal{L}^{-1}(U_{P_{1}})\times\mathcal{L}^{-1}(U_{P_{1}})

and

H^{*}_{c}(\mathcal{L}^{-1}(U_{P}),\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})\cong H^{*}_{c}(\mathcal{L}^{-1}(U_{P_{1}}),\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}})\otimes H^{*}_{c}(\mathcal{L}^{-1}(U_{P_{2}}),\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}),

as
\mathbb{C}[L_{1}^{F}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[L_{2}^{F}]
-modules, from which we deduce that

R^{G}_{L}(M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2})=R^{G_{1}}_{L_{1}}(M_{1})\boxtimes R^{G_{2}}_{L_{2}}(M_{2}).

∎

Remark 5.2.4
.

Consider an
F
-stable Levi subgroup
L^{\prime}\supseteq L
and a linear character
\theta:(L^{\prime})^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
. By restriction, we can consider it as a character
\theta:L^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{C}(L^{F})
, we have an identity
R^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(\theta f)=\theta R^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(f)
and therefore, by Lemma
5.2.3
(1), an equality
R^{G}_{L}(\theta f)=R^{G}_{L^{\prime}}(\theta R^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(f))
.

5.3
Unipotent characters

Fix now an
F
-stable maximal torus
T\subseteq G
. We follow the notations of
[
18
, Chapter 11]
.

Denote by
W
the Weyl group
W_{G}(T)
and by
\widetilde{W}
the semidirect group
W\rtimes\langle F\rangle
, where
\langle F\rangle
is the group generated by the finite order automorphism induced by
F
on
W
.

Denote by
\mathcal{C}(WF)
the vector space of functions
f:W\to\mathbb{C}
constant on
F
-conjugacy classes. Equivalenty, a function
f\in\mathcal{C}(WF)
can be seen as
a function on the coset
WF\subseteq\widetilde{W}
, invariant under
W
-conjugation.

The vector space
\mathcal{C}(WF)
is endowed with the Hermitian product defined as

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{WF}=\dfrac{1}{|W|}\sum_{w\in WF}f(w)\overline{g(w)},

for
f,g\in\mathcal{C}(WF).

Example 5.3.1
.

Let
G=(\Gl_{n})_{d}
and
T
be the torus of Example
3.2.4
. In this case, the morphism
\psi:\mathcal{C}(S_{n})\to\mathcal{C}(WF)
defined as
\psi(f)(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{d})=f(\sigma_{d}\cdots\sigma_{1})
is an isomorphism. It is not difficult to see that the isomorphism
\psi
is an isometry too.

In
[
18
, Chapter 11.6]
, it is shown how to associate to each
\chi\in(W^{\vee})^{F}
a function in
\mathcal{C}(WF)
, which we denote by
\tilde{\chi}.
This association is isometric with respect to the natural Hermitian products on both sides. The elements
\{\tilde{\chi}\}_{\chi\in(W^{\vee})^{F}}
form moreover a basis of
\mathcal{C}(WF)
.

Remark 5.3.2
.

Consider the group
G
and the torus
T
of Example
5.3.1
above. An irreducible character
\chi\in W^{\vee}
is determined by partitions
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{d}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
such that
\chi=\chi^{\lambda^{1}}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\chi^{\lambda^{d}}
. The character
\chi
is
F
-stable if and only if
\lambda^{1}=\cdots=\lambda^{d}=\lambda
, so that
(W^{\vee})^{F}
is in bijection with
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.

For
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, consider the associated function
\widetilde{(\chi^{\lambda})^{\boxtimes d}}\in\mathcal{C}(WF)
.
It is possible to check that
\psi^{-1}(\widetilde{(\chi^{\lambda})^{\boxtimes d}})=\chi^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}(S_{n})
.

In
[
18
, Chapter 11.6]
for
f\in\mathcal{C}(WF)
, it is defined a class function
R_{f}:G^{F}\to\mathbb{C}
as

R_{f}\coloneqq\dfrac{1}{|W|}\sum_{w\in W}f(w)R^{G}_{T_{w}}(1).

(5.3.1)

In
locus cit
, it shown that the map
f\to R_{f}
induces an isometry
\mathcal{C}(WF)\to\mathcal{C}(G^{F})
. In particular, the elements
\{R_{\tilde{\chi}}\}_{\chi\in(W^{\vee})^{F}}
have norm
1
and are pairwise orthogonal in
\mathcal{C}(G^{F})
.

Consider now an
F
-stable Levi subgroup
L\supseteq T
and the corresponding Weyl group
W_{L}\coloneqq W_{L}(T)
which is an
F
-stable subgroup of
W
. Define the induction map
\Ind_{W_{L}F}^{WF}:\mathcal{C}(W_{L}F)\to\mathcal{C}(WF)
as

\displaystyle\Ind_{W_{L}F}^{WF}(f)(w)=\dfrac{1}{|W_{L}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in W_{L}\\
h^{-1}wF(h)\in W_{L}\end{subarray}}f(h^{-1}wF(h)).

In
[
18
, Proposition 11.6.6]
it is shown the following Lemma.

Lemma 5.3.3
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{C}(W_{L}F)
, we have:

R^{G}_{L}(R_{f})=R_{\Ind_{W_{L}F}^{WF}(f)}

(5.3.2)

Let
G=(\Gl_{n})_{d}
and
T
be the torus considered above. In
[
18
, Chapter 11.7]
, it is shown the following Lemma.

Lemma 5.3.4
.

For each
\chi\in(W^{\vee})^{F}
, the class function
R_{\tilde{\chi}}
is an irreducible character of
(\Gl_{n})_{d}^{F}
.

The irreducible characters of this form are called
unipotent characters
. In particular, for every
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, there is a corresponding irreducible character
R_{\widetilde{\chi_{\lambda}^{\boxtimes d}}}\in\mathcal{C}((\Gl_{n})_{d}^{F})
, which we will denote by
R_{\lambda}
.

From Lemma
5.2.3
(2), we deduce that Lemma
5.3.4
is true more generally for any group
G
of the form
(\Gl_{n_{1}})_{d_{1}}\times\cdots(\Gl_{n_{r}})_{d_{r}}
. In this case, the unipotent characters are in bijection with the multipartitions
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{P}_{n_{r}}
and we denote by
R_{\bm{\lambda}}
the associated irreducible unipotent character.

Consider now a group
G=(\Gl_{n_{1}})_{d_{1}}\times\cdots(\Gl_{n_{r}})_{d_{r}}
. An argument similar to that at the end of Example
3.3.2
shows that all
F
-stable Levi subgroups are isomorphic to a group of the form
(\Gl_{n^{\prime}_{1}})_{d^{\prime}_{1}}\times\cdots(\Gl_{n^{\prime}_{s}})_{d^{\prime}_{s}}
. By eq.(
5.3.2
), we deduce the following Proposition.

Proposition 5.3.5
.

Let
G=(\Gl_{n_{1}})_{d_{1}}\times\cdots(\Gl_{n_{r}})_{d_{r}}
and
L\subseteq G
an
F
-stable Levi subgroup such that

(L,F)\cong(\Gl_{n^{\prime}_{1}})_{d^{\prime}_{1}}\times\cdots(\Gl_{n^{\prime}_{s}})_{d^{\prime}_{s}}.

For any
\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}_{s}}
, the character
R^{G}_{L}(R_{\bm{\mu}})
belongs to the vector space spanned by the unipotent characters of
G^{F}
.

Lastly, consider an
F
-stable Levi subgroup
L\subseteq G
, a class function
R_{f}\in\mathcal{C}(L^{F})
for
f\in\mathcal{C}(W_{L}F)
and a linear character
\theta:L^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Fix a central element
\gamma\in G^{F}
. Notice that
\gamma\in L^{F}
too. The Mackey’s formula
[
18
, Proposition 10.1.2]
implies the following Proposition.

Proposition 5.3.6
.

We have an equality:

R^{G}_{L}(\theta R_{f})(\gamma)=R^{G}_{L}(\theta R_{f})(e)\theta(\gamma).

(5.3.3)

5.4
Characters of tori and graphs

Consider the group
\Gl_{\alpha}
and fix an
F
-stable maximal torus
T\subseteq\Gl_{\alpha}
. We follow the notations of
section

3.6
.

Recall that we have the dual root system
\Phi^{\vee}\subseteq Y_{*}(T)
which is endowed with a canonical bijection
\Phi\leftrightarrow\Phi^{\vee}
and that, for each
h\neq j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, we denote by
\epsilon^{\vee}_{h,j}
the element associated to
\epsilon_{h,j}

through this bijection.

Consider now a character
\theta:T^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
. In this paragraph, we show how to associate an admissible graph
\Gamma_{\theta}
with vertices
\mathcal{B}
to the character
\theta
.

In
[
18
, Proposition 11.7.1]
, it is shown that there exists a canonical short exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{*}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{*}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

In particular, the character
\theta:T^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
induces by restriction a morphism
\widetilde{\theta}:Y_{*}(T)\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

The graph
\Gamma_{\theta}
is defined as follows. The set of the vertices of
\Gamma_{\theta}
is
\mathcal{B}=\{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{m}\}
and, for each
h>j
, there is an edge between
\epsilon_{h}
and
\epsilon_{j}
if and only if

\epsilon_{h,j}^{\vee}\in\Ker(\widetilde{\theta}).

From Remark
3.6.1
, we deduce the following Lemma.

Lemma 5.4.1
.

For any
\theta:T^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
, the graph
\Gamma_{\theta}
is admissible.

In particular, from Lemma
3.6.7
, there exists a unique admissible subtorus
S_{\theta}\subseteq\Gl_{\alpha}
such that
\Gamma_{S_{\theta}}=\Gamma_{\theta}
.

We will denote by
L_{\theta}=C_{\Gl_{|\alpha|}}(S_{\theta})
and by
\widetilde{L_{\theta}}=L_{\theta}\cap\Gl_{\alpha}
. The Levi subgroup
L_{\theta}
is the
connected centralizer
of
\theta
in
\Gl_{|\alpha|}
, defined in
[
16
, Definition 5.19]
and
\widetilde{L_{\theta}}
is the connected centralizer of
\theta
in
\Gl_{\alpha}
.

Example 5.4.2
.

Consider the subset
I=\{1,2,3,4\}
, the dimension vector
\alpha=(2,1,1,1)
and the torus
T
of diagonal matrices of Example
3.6.12
. Notice that

T^{F}=\Biggl\{\left(\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\mu\end{pmatrix},\gamma,\delta,\eta\right)\ |\ \lambda,\mu,\gamma,\delta,\eta\in\ \mathbb{F}_{q}^{*}\Biggr\}.

Consider
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*},\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}
and let

\theta_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}:T^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}

defined as

\theta_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}(\lambda,\mu,\gamma,\delta,\eta)=\beta_{1}(\lambda\gamma\delta\eta)\beta_{2}(\mu).

If
\beta_{1}\neq\beta_{2}
, the graph
\Gamma_{\theta_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}}
is
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{2}}

and the admissible torus
S_{\theta_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}}
is therefore the torus
S_{1}
of Example
3.4.8
.

If
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}
, the graph
\Gamma_{\theta_{\beta_{1},\beta_{1}}}
is
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{2}}

i.e. the complete graph with
5
vertices and the admissible torus
S_{\theta_{\beta_{1},\beta_{1}}}
is thus
Z_{\alpha}
.

From
[
16
, Proposition 5.11]
, we deduce the following Proposition.

Proposition 5.4.3
.

For any
\theta:T^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
, the character
\theta
can be extended to a character
\theta:L_{\theta}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

5.5
Characters of tori and Levi subgroups of the finite general linear groups

5.5.1
Characters of Levi subgroups

Consider now
G=\Gl_{\alpha}
, an admissible torus
S
, the associated Levi subgroups
L_{S}=C_{\Gl_{|\alpha|}}(S)\subseteq\Gl_{|\alpha|}
and
\widetilde{L_{S}}=L_{S}\cap\Gl_{\alpha}
. Let
T
be an
F
-stable maximal torus
T\subseteq\Gl_{\alpha}
such that
\widetilde{L_{S}}\supseteq T\supseteq S
.

We use the notations introduced in
section

3.6.3
,
section

5.4
for the graphs associated to
T,S,\theta
in this situation.

Consider now a character
\theta:L_{S}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
. By restriction, we obtain a character
\theta:T^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
, from which we define an associated admissible torus
S_{\theta}
and the corresponding Levi subgroups
L_{\theta},\widetilde{L_{\theta}}
, as in
section

5.4
.

In general, we have that
S_{\theta}\leq S
, i.e.
L_{\theta}\supseteq L_{S}
(or equivalently
\Gamma_{\theta}\leq\Gamma_{S}
). In this case,
\Gamma_{\theta}=\Gamma_{S}
if and only if
\widetilde{L_{\theta}}=\widetilde{L_{S}}
.

As recalled in Proposition
5.4.3
, the character
\theta
can be extended to the connected centralizer
\theta:L_{\theta}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Since
L_{\theta}\supseteq\widetilde{L_{\theta}}
, in particular,
\theta
can be extended to a character
\theta:\widetilde{L_{\theta}}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

Conversely, for each character
\theta:T^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that
L_{\theta}\supseteq L_{S}
(i.e.
\Gamma_{\theta}\leq\Gamma_{S})
, the character
\theta
can be first extended to
\theta:L_{\theta}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
and then restricted to obtain a linear character
\theta:L_{S}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
and
\theta:\widetilde{L_{S}}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

We obtain therefore the following correspondence.

Proposition 5.5.1
.

There are bijections:

\Hom(L_{S}^{F},\mathbb{C}^{*})\leftrightarrow\ \{\theta\in\Hom(T^{F},\mathbb{C}^{*})\ |\ \Gamma_{\theta}\leq\Gamma_{S}\}\leftrightarrow\{\theta\in\Hom(\widetilde{L_{S}}^{F},\mathbb{C}^{*})\ |\ \Gamma_{\theta}\leq\Gamma_{S}\}.

(5.5.1)

Lastly, we give the following definition of a
reduced
character.

Definition 5.5.2
.

Given an
F
-stable Levi subgroup
L\subseteq\Gl_{\alpha}
and a character
\theta:L^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
, we say that
\theta
is reduced if there exists an admissible
F
-stable subtorus
S\subseteq\Gl_{\alpha}
and an
F
-stable subtorus
T\supseteq S
such that

•

L=\widetilde{L_{S}}

•

For the connected centralizer
L_{\theta}
defined from
T
, we have
\widetilde{L_{\theta}}=\widetilde{L_{S}}
.

5.5.2
Reduced characters and connected centralizers

Let now
G=\Gl_{n}
(i.e.
|I|=1
) and consider an
F
-stable Levi subgroup
L\subseteq\Gl_{n}
and a linear character
\theta:L^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
. The Levi subgroup
L
contains an
F
-stable maximal torus
T
.

From
\theta,T
, we determine the connected centralizer
L_{\theta}\supseteq L
, as defined above. In this case, the character
\theta
is reduced if
L_{\theta}=L
.

Remark 5.5.3
.

While the connected centralizer
L_{\theta}
does depend on the choice of the torus
T
, from
[
16
, Proposition 5.11(ii), Proposition 5.20]
we deduce that for any two
F
-stable maximal tori
T,T^{\prime}\subseteq L
and the corresponding connected centralizers
L_{\theta},L^{\prime}_{\theta}
, there exists an element
g\in\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
such that
gL_{\theta}g^{-1}=L_{\theta}^{\prime}
.

In particular, the property of being reduced does not depend on the choice of the maximal torus
T
.

We will now describe the connected centralizers
L_{\theta}
for certain Levi subgroups
L\subseteq\Gl_{n}
and certain linear characters
\theta:L^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
. This description is going to be useful both for recalling the construction of irreducible characters of
\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
in
section

5.6
and for the proof of Lemma
6.6.3
, which is the key technical point to prove our main result about multiplicative quiver stacks over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, Theorem
6.5.1
.

For any two positive integers
r,d
such that
r|d
, the norm map
N_{\mathbb{F}^{*}_{q^{d}}/\mathbb{F}^{*}_{q^{r}}}:\mathbb{F}^{*}_{q^{d}}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{r}}^{*}
induces by precomposition an injective homomorphism

\Gamma_{r,d}\coloneqq\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q^{r}}^{*},\mathbb{C}^{*})\to\Hom(\mathbb{F}^{*}_{q^{d}},\mathbb{C}^{*}).

We denote by
\Gamma
the inductive limit via these maps

\Gamma\coloneqq\varinjlim\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q^{d}}^{*},\mathbb{C}^{*}).

Notice that, for any
n\geq 1
, we can view
\Hom(\mathbb{F}^{*}_{q^{n}},\mathbb{C}^{*})
as a subgroup of
\Gamma
through the universal maps of the limit. The Frobenius morphism acts by precomposition on each term
\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q^{d}}^{*},\mathbb{C}^{*})
(i.e.
F(\gamma)=\gamma\circ F
) and so defines a morphism
F:\Gamma\to\Gamma
.

Consider the Levi subgroup

L=(\Gl_{n_{1}})_{d_{1}}\times\cdots\times(\Gl_{n_{r}})_{d_{r}}

with
n_{1},\dots,n_{r},

d_{1},\dots,d_{r}
positive integers such that
d_{1}n_{1}+\cdots+d_{r}n_{r}=n
and let
T
be the maximal torus

(T_{n_{1}})_{d_{1}}\times\cdots(T_{n_{r}})_{d_{r}}.

The group
L^{F}
is isomorphic to
\Gl_{n_{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{d_{1}}})\times\cdots\times\Gl_{n_{r}}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{d_{r}}})
. A character
\theta:L^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
corresponds thus to an element
(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{r})\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q^{d_{1}}}^{*},\mathbb{C}^{*})\times\cdots\times\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q^{d_{r}}}^{*},\mathbb{C}^{*})
such that

\displaystyle\theta(M_{1},\dots,M_{r})=\prod_{j=1}^{r}\theta_{j}(\det(M_{j}))

with
M_{j}\in\Gl_{n_{j}}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{d_{j}}})
. We have the following Lemma

Lemma 5.5.4
.

The character
\theta
is reduced if and only if the
F
-orbits of
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{r}
inside
\Gamma
have length
d_{1},\dots,d_{r}
respectively and are pairwise disjoint.

Proof.

Notice that, for any
h\in\{1,\dots,n\}
there exist unique
i_{h}\in\{1,\dots,r\}
and
j_{h}\in\{1,\dots,d_{i_{h}}\}
such that

\sum_{s=1}^{i_{h}-1}d_{s}n_{s}<h\leq\sum_{s=1}^{i_{h}}d_{s}n_{s}

and

\sum_{s=1}^{i_{h}-1}d_{s}n_{s}+n_{i_{h}}(j_{h}-1)<h\leq\sum_{s=1}^{i_{h}-1}d_{s}n_{s}+n_{i_{h}}j_{h}.

For
h_{1},h_{2}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, we have that

\widetilde{\theta}(\epsilon_{h_{1},h_{2}}^{\vee})=1

if and only if

\theta_{i_{h_{1}}}^{q^{j_{h_{1}}}}=\theta_{i_{h_{2}}}^{q^{j_{h_{2}}}}

as elements of
\Gamma
, from which we deduce the Lemma above.

∎

Consider now two
F
-stable Levi subgroups
L\subseteq\Gl_{n}
and
L^{\prime}\subseteq\Gl_{n^{\prime}}
and the Levi subgroup
M=L\times L^{\prime}\subseteq\Gl_{m}
embedded block diagonally, where
m=n+n^{\prime}
.

Assume that
L=(\Gl_{n_{1}})_{d_{1}}\times\cdots\times(\Gl_{n_{r}})_{d_{r}}
and
L^{\prime}=(\Gl_{n^{\prime}_{1}})_{d_{1}^{\prime}}\times\cdots\times(\Gl_{n^{\prime}_{s}})_{d^{\prime}_{s}}
and consider two reduced characters
\theta:L^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
and
\theta^{\prime}:(L^{\prime})^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
corresponding to
(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{r}),(\theta_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\theta^{\prime}_{s})
where
\theta_{i}\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q^{d_{i}}}^{*},\mathbb{C}^{*}),\theta^{\prime}_{j}\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q^{d^{\prime}_{j}}}^{*},\mathbb{C}^{*})
for
i=1,\dots,r
,
j=1,\dots,s
.

Consider the character

\gamma=\theta\times\theta^{\prime}:M^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}.

Its connected centralizer
M_{\gamma}
admits the following description. For
i\in\{1,\dots,r\}
, consider the subset
J_{i}\subseteq\{1,\dots,s\}
defined as

J_{i}\coloneqq\bigl\{j\in\{1,\dots,s\}\ |\ d^{\prime}_{j}=d_{i}\text{ and the }F\text{-orbits
inside }\Gamma\text{ of }\theta_{i},\theta^{\prime}_{j}\text{ have nonempty intersection}\bigr\}.

We have that either
J_{i}=\varnothing
or
J_{i}=\{j_{i}\}
for an element
j_{i}\in\{1,\dots,s\}
, since the characters
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
are both reduced. Denote by
I^{\prime}\subseteq\{1,\dots,r\}
the subset
I^{\prime}\coloneqq\{i\ |\ J_{i}=\varnothing\}
and by
J^{\prime}\subseteq\{1,\dots,s\}
the subset
\displaystyle J^{\prime}=\{1,\dots,s\}\setminus\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{r}J_{i}
.

In a similar way to the one used to prove Lemma
5.5.4
, we see that any connected centralizer
M_{\gamma}
is
\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugated to the Levi subgroup

M^{\prime}_{\gamma}=\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I^{\prime}}(\Gl_{n_{i}})_{d_{i}}\prod_{j\in J^{\prime}}(\Gl_{n^{\prime}_{j}})_{d^{\prime}_{j}}\prod_{i\in(I^{\prime})^{c}}(\Gl_{n_{i}+n^{\prime}_{j_{i}}})_{d_{i}}.

Through this conjugation, the character
\gamma
corresponds to the character
\gamma:(M^{\prime}_{\gamma})^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
associated to
((\theta_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}},(\theta^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in J^{\prime}},(\theta_{i})_{i\in(I^{\prime})^{c}})
.

5.6
Irreducible characters of finite general linear group

In this paragraph, we quickly recall how to build the character table of the groups
\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. We start from the following Lemma, which will also be needed later. Its proof is a consequence of
[
18
, Lemma 11.4.3,Lemma 11.4.4]
and
[
44
, Theorem 8]
.

Lemma 5.6.1
.

Consider
G=\Gl_{\alpha}
, an
F
-stable Levi subgroup
L\subseteq G

and two characters
R_{\varphi_{1}},R_{\varphi_{2}}\in\mathcal{C}(L^{F})
with
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(W_{L}F)
. Let
\theta:L^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
be a reduced character.

We have that:

\langle R^{G}_{L}(\theta R_{\varphi_{1}}),R^{G}_{L}(\theta R_{\varphi_{2}})\rangle_{G^{F}}=\langle R_{\varphi_{1}},R_{\varphi_{2}}\rangle_{L^{F}}.

Notice in particular that if
\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}=\tilde{\psi}
with
\psi\in(W_{L}^{\vee})^{F}
, from Lemma
5.3.4
, we have that

\langle R^{G}_{L}(\theta R_{\tilde{\psi}}),R^{G}_{L}(\theta R_{\tilde{\psi}})\rangle=\langle R_{\tilde{\psi}},R_{\tilde{\psi}}\rangle=1.

In particular, the character
R^{G}_{L}(\theta R_{\tilde{\psi}})
is a virtual irreducible character, i.e. an irreducible character up to a sign.

From these remarks, in
[
45
, Theorem 3.2]
, it is shown the following Theorem.

Theorem 5.6.2
.

For an irreducible character
\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
, we have

\chi=\epsilon_{G}\epsilon_{L}R^{G}_{L}(\theta R_{\tilde{\varphi}}),

where
L
is an
F
-stable Levi subgroup,
\varphi\in(W^{\vee}_{L})^{F}
and
\theta:L^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
is a reduced character.

Two characters
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
with associated data
(L_{1},\theta_{1},\varphi_{1})
and
(L_{2},\theta_{2},\varphi_{2})
are equal if and only if the triples
(L_{1},\theta_{1},\varphi_{1}),(L_{2},\theta_{2},\varphi_{2})
are
\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugated.

For an irreducible character
\chi
with associated datum
(L,\theta,\varphi)
, we will call the couple
(L,\theta)
the
semisimple part
of
\chi
. This is well defined up to
\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy.

Remark 5.6.3
.

Let
G=\Gl_{n}
and consider now an
F
-stable Levi subgroup
L
, a character
\gamma:L^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
(not necessarily reduced) and a unipotent irreducible character
R_{\tilde{\psi}}
for
\psi\in(W^{\vee}_{L})^{F}
.

Let
L_{\gamma}
be a connected centralizer of
\gamma
. By Remark
5.2.4
, we
have an equality

R^{G}_{L}(\gamma R_{\tilde{\psi}})=R^{G}_{L_{\gamma}}(\gamma R^{L_{\gamma}}_{L}(R_{\tilde{\psi}})).

(5.6.1)

Notice that from Proposition
5.3.5
, we have that
R^{L_{\gamma}}_{L}(R_{\tilde{\psi}})
belongs to the vector space spanned by
the unipotent characters of
L_{\gamma}
. We deduce thus the following Proposition.

Proposition 5.6.4
.

For
G=\Gl_{n}
, any
F
-stable Levi subgroup
L\subseteq G
, any
\gamma:L^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
and any
\psi\in(W^{\vee}_{L})^{F}
, the character
R^{G}_{L}(\gamma R_{\tilde{\psi}})
belongs to the vector space spanned by the
irreducible characters of
\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
with semisimple part
(L_{\gamma},\gamma)
.

From Proposition
5.3.6
, we deduce in particular the following Proposition:

Proposition 5.6.5
.

Given
\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}^{\vee}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
with
\chi=\epsilon_{G}\epsilon_{L}R^{G}_{L}(\theta R_{\tilde{\psi}})
and a central element
\eta=(\eta_{i}I_{\alpha_{i}})_{i\in I}
, we have

\dfrac{\chi(\eta)}{\chi(e)}=\theta(\eta).

(5.6.2)

5.7
Type of an irreducible character

Let
\chi\in\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
with associated datum
(L,\theta,\varphi)
. Up to
\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy,
L
is equal to
(\Gl_{n_{1}})_{d_{1}}\times\cdots\times(\Gl_{n_{r}})_{d_{r}}
and
\varphi=R_{\bm{\lambda}}
for
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{r})\in\mathcal{P}_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{P}_{n_{r}}
a multipartition. Up to reordering, the
(d_{i},\lambda_{i})
s define a type

\omega_{\chi}\coloneqq(d_{1},\lambda_{1})\dots(d_{r},\lambda_{r})\in\mathbb{T}_{n}

which is called the type of the irreducible character
\chi
.

Example 5.7.1
.

Consider a partition
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{h})\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
and the associated split Levi subgroup
L_{\mu}=\Gl_{\mu_{1}}\times\Gl_{\mu_{2}}\times\cdots\times\Gl_{\mu_{h}}\subseteq\Gl_{n}
. For each reduced character
\theta:L_{\mu}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
, the type of the character
R^{G}_{L_{\mu}}(\theta)
is
(1,(\mu_{1}))\cdots(1,(\mu_{h}))
.

In a similar way, for any finite set
I
and any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
, to each irreducible character
\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
, we can associate a multitype
\omega_{\chi}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
in the following way.

Let
\chi=\epsilon_{\Gl_{\alpha}}\epsilon_{L}R^{\Gl_{\alpha}}_{L}(R_{\tilde{\varphi}}\theta)
, where
\theta:L^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
is a reduced character and
R_{\tilde{\varphi}}
is a unipotent character of
L^{F}
with
\varphi\in(W^{\vee}_{L})^{F}
.

Consider an
F
-stable torus
T\subseteq L
and the restricted character
\theta:T^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
. As explained in
section

5.4
, this determines a Levi subgroup
L_{\theta}\subseteq\Gl_{|\alpha|}
with admissible center
S_{\theta}\subseteq T

and such that
L_{\theta}\cap\Gl_{\alpha}=L
, since
\theta
is reduced.

Consider the semisimple multitype
[S_{\theta}]=(d_{1},(1^{\beta_{1}}))\dots(d_{r},(1^{\beta_{r}}))
. As in the case of
\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, the character
\varphi
determines multipartition
\bm{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\bm{\lambda}_{r}\in\mathcal{P}^{I}
such that
|\bm{\lambda}_{j}|=\beta_{j}
. Up to reordering, the
(d_{i},\bm{\lambda}_{i})
s define a multitype

\omega_{\chi}\coloneqq(d_{1},\bm{\lambda}_{1})\dots(d_{r},\bm{\lambda}_{r}).

For
\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{I}
and
\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
, we will use the notation
\chi\sim\omega
if
\omega_{\chi}=\omega
.

Example 5.7.2
.

Let
I=\{1,2,3,4\}
and
\alpha=(2,1,1,1)
. Let
T\subseteq\Gl_{2}
be the
F
-stable torus of diagonal matrices, consider
\beta\neq\gamma\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*},\mathbb{C}^{*})
and the associated character
(\beta,\gamma):T^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Let
\chi
be the character
\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}

\chi=R^{G}_{T}((\beta,\gamma))\boxtimes\gamma\circ\det\boxtimes\gamma\circ\det\boxtimes\gamma\circ\det.

Let
\beta_{1}=(1,1,1,1)
and
\beta_{2}=(1,0,0,0)
. The associated multitype is

\omega_{\chi}=(1,(\beta_{1}))(1,(\beta_{2})).

Remark 5.7.3
.

Given
\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
, consider an irreducible character
\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
of type
\omega
. Fix
S\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
such that
[S]=\omega^{ss}
(for instance
S=S_{\omega}
). We can assume then that

\chi=\epsilon_{\widetilde{L_{S}}}\epsilon_{\Gl_{\alpha}}R^{\Gl_{\alpha}}_{\widetilde{L_{S}}}(\theta R_{\widetilde{\varphi}})

with
\theta:\widetilde{L_{S}}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that
S_{\theta}=S
and
R_{\widetilde{\varphi}}
a unipotent character of
\widetilde{L_{S}}^{F}
. Notice that for any
\gamma:\widetilde{L_{S}}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that
S_{\gamma}=S
, the character
\epsilon_{\widetilde{L_{S}}}\epsilon_{\Gl_{\alpha}}R^{\Gl_{\alpha}}_{\widetilde{L_{S}}}(\gamma R_{\widetilde{\varphi}})
is irreducible and of type
\omega
.

The map from
\{\gamma:\widetilde{L_{S}}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}\ |\ S_{\gamma}=S\}
to
\{\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}\text{ of multitype }\omega\}
which sends
\gamma
to
\epsilon_{\widetilde{L_{S}}}\epsilon_{\Gl_{\alpha}}R^{\Gl_{\alpha}}_{\widetilde{L_{S}}}(\gamma R_{\widetilde{\varphi}})
is surjective and its fibers have cardinality
w(\omega)
, see for example
[
41
, Proof of Theorem 2.2]
for the analogous statement for conjugacy classes of
\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

Recall that the value
\dfrac{\chi(e)}{|\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}
for
\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
depends only on the multitype of
\chi.
More precisely, for a partition
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}
, let
H_{\lambda}(t)
be the
hook polynomial

\displaystyle H_{\lambda}(t)\coloneqq\prod_{s\in\lambda}(1-t^{h(s)}).

For a multipartition
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda^{i})_{i\in I}\in\mathcal{P}^{I}
, we define
\displaystyle H_{\bm{\lambda}}(t)\coloneqq\prod_{i\in I}H_{\lambda^{i}}(t)
. Given a multitype
\omega=(d_{1},\bm{\lambda}_{1})\dots(d_{r},\bm{\lambda}_{r})
, define
H_{\omega}^{\vee}(t)
as

H^{\vee}_{\omega}(t)\coloneqq\dfrac{(-1)^{f(\omega)}}{q^{\left(\sum_{i\in I}\frac{\alpha_{i}(\alpha_{i}-1)}{2}-n(\omega)\right)}\prod_{j=1}^{r}H_{\bm{\lambda}_{j}}(t^{d_{j}})},

(5.7.1)

where if
|\bm{\lambda}_{1}|=\beta_{1},\dots,|\bm{\lambda}_{r}|=\beta_{r}
, then

f(\omega)\coloneqq\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{r}|\beta_{j}|

and

n(\omega)\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}n(\bm{\lambda}_{j}).

We have the following Proposition (see
[
46
, IV, 6.7]
).

Proposition 5.7.4
.

For any
\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
, we have:

\dfrac{\chi(e)}{|\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}=H_{\omega_{\chi}}^{\vee}(q).

(5.7.2)

5.8
Multiplicative parameters

Given an element
\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i\in I}\in(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*})^{I}
and
\delta\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
, we define

\eta^{\delta}\coloneqq\prod_{i\in I}\eta_{i}^{\delta_{i}}.

We denote by
\mathcal{H}_{\eta}
the subset of
\mathbb{N}^{I}
defined as

\mathcal{H}_{\eta}\coloneqq\{\delta\in\mathbb{N}^{I}\ |\ \eta^{\delta}=1\}

and, for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
, we denote by
\mathcal{H}_{\eta,\alpha}
the intersection
\mathcal{H}_{\eta,\alpha}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}_{\eta}\cap\mathbb{N}^{I}_{\leq\alpha}
. For an admissible torus
S\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
, we say that
S
is of level
\eta
if it is of level
\mathcal{H}_{\eta,\alpha}
.

Fix now
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
. We still denote by
\eta
the central element
\eta\coloneqq(\eta_{i}I_{\alpha_{i}})\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Assume now to have fixed, for each
S\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
, an
F
-stable maximal torus
\Gl_{\alpha}\supseteq T_{S}\supseteq S
in such a way that if
S\leq S^{\prime}
then
T_{S}=T_{S^{\prime}}
. Define then the functions
g_{\eta},f_{\eta}:\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{C}
as

g_{\eta}(S)\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta:T_{S}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}\\
\Gamma_{\theta}=\Gamma_{S}\end{subarray}}\theta(\eta)

and

f_{\eta}(S)\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta:T_{S}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}\\
\Gamma_{\theta}\leq\Gamma_{S}\end{subarray}}\theta(\eta).

The understanding of the functions
f_{\eta},g_{\eta}
is a key part of the proof of Theorem
5.9.3
, which is going to be the main technical result needed to prove Theorem
7.2.9
.

By Identity (
3.5.6
), we have

g_{\eta}(S)=\sum_{S^{\prime}\leq S}\mu(S^{\prime},S)f_{\eta}(S^{\prime})

(5.8.1)

Notice that by the bijection of eq.(
5.5.1
), for each
S\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
, we have

f_{\eta}(S)=\sum_{\theta:L_{S}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}}\theta(\eta).

Fix now
S
with associated semisimple multitype
[S]=(d_{1},\beta_{1})\dots(d_{r},\beta_{r})
, where
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{r}\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
. Notice that there exists
h\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
such that
\displaystyle hSh^{-1}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}(Z_{\beta_{j}})_{d_{j}}
and so

\displaystyle hL_{S}h^{-1}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}(\Gl_{|\beta_{j}|})_{d_{j}}.

In particular, a character
\theta:L_{S}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
, thorugh the conjugation by
h
, corresponds to an element
(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{r})\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}^{*}_{q^{d_{1}}},\mathbb{C}^{*})\times\cdots\times\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q^{d_{r}}}^{*},\mathbb{C}^{*})
such that

\displaystyle\theta(M_{1},\dots,M_{r})=\prod_{j=1}^{r}\theta_{j}(\det(M_{j}))

with
M_{j}\in\Gl_{|\beta_{j}|}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{d_{j}}})
.

As the element
\eta\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is central, we have the following equality

\theta(\eta)=\prod_{j=1}^{r}\theta_{j}(\eta^{\beta_{j}}).

(5.8.2)

In particular, eq.(
5.8.2
) implies that
f_{\eta}(S)\neq 0
if and only if
\eta^{\beta_{j}}=1
for each
j=1,\dots,r
, i.e. if and only if
S
is of level
\eta
. From eq.(
5.8.1
), we therefore deduce that, for each
S\in\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}
, we have

g_{\eta}(S)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\leq S\\
\text{ of level }\eta\end{subarray}}|\Hom(L_{S^{\prime}}^{F},\mathbb{C}^{*})|\mu(S^{\prime},S)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\leq S\\
\text{ of level }\eta\end{subarray}}P_{[S^{\prime}]}(q)\mu(S^{\prime},S).

(5.8.3)

5.9
Dual Log compatiblity

Consider a family of class functions
\{c_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}(\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\}
.

Definition 5.9.1
.

The family
\{c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
is said to be
dual Log compatible
if the product
\langle c_{\alpha},\chi\rangle
depends only on the multitype of
\chi
and the value of
\langle c_{\alpha},\chi\rangle
for
\chi\sim\omega
is of the form
C_{\omega}(q)
where
\{C_{\omega}(t)\in{\mathbb{Q}}(t)\}_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{I}}
is a family of rational functions such that for any
d_{1},\dots,d_{r}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\omega_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{\beta_{1}},\dots,\omega_{r}\in\mathbb{T}_{\beta_{r}}
such that
\psi_{d_{1}}(\omega_{1})\ast\cdots\ast\psi_{d_{r}}(\omega_{r})=\omega
, it holds

C_{\omega_{1}}(t^{d_{1}})\cdots C_{\omega_{r}}(t^{d_{r}})\prod_{j=1}^{r}H^{\vee}_{\omega_{j}}(t^{d_{j}})=C_{\omega}(t)H_{\omega}^{\vee}(t).

(5.9.1)

i.e. the family
\{C_{\omega}(t)H^{\vee}_{\omega}(t)\}_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{I}}
is Log compatible.

For each
\omega\in\mathbb{T}
, denote by
\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{\omega}(t)\coloneqq C_{\omega}(t)H^{\vee}_{\omega}(t)
. The family
\{\widetilde{C}_{\omega}\}_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{I}}
is therefore Log compatible. For each
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
and for each
\eta\in(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*})^{I}
, we will denote by
\widetilde{C}_{\alpha,\eta}(t)
the polynomial
\widetilde{C}_{\alpha,\mathcal{H}_{\eta,\alpha}}(t)
introduced in eq.(
4.0.1
).

Example 5.9.2
.

Let
I=\{\cdot\}
and, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
f_{n}:\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{C}
be the class function

f_{n}(h)\coloneqq\#\{(x,y)\in\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\times\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\ |\ [x,y]=h\}

for
h\in\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. For any
\chi\in\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
of type
\omega
, it holds
\langle f_{n},\chi\rangle=\dfrac{1}{H^{\vee}_{\omega}(q)}
. More generally for any finite group
G
and any irreducible character
\chi\in G^{\vee}
we have:

\sum_{(a,b)\in G^{2}}\chi([a,b])=\dfrac{|G|}{\chi(1)}.

(5.9.2)

This equality is obtained by applying Schur’s lemma in a classical way as explained in
[
30
, Paragraph 2.3]
. Notice that, from the identity
\langle f_{n},\chi\rangle=\dfrac{1}{H^{\vee}_{\omega}(q)}
, we immediately deduce that the family
\{f_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is dual Log compatible and, more precisely, for each
\omega
, the associated function
\widetilde{F_{\omega}}(t)
is equal to
1
.

The notion of dual Log compatible families will be one of the key elements to show Theorem
7.2.9
about E-series of non-generic character stacks. Their importance comes from the following Theorem.

Theorem 5.9.3
.

For any dual Log compatible family
\{c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
and any
\eta\in(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*})^{I}
, there is an equality

\dfrac{c_{\alpha}(\eta)}{|\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}=\Coeff_{\alpha}\left(\Plexp\left(\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{H}_{\eta}}\widetilde{C}_{\beta,gen}(q)y^{\beta}\right)\right)

(5.9.3)

Proof.

By Theorem
4.1.2
and the Log compatibility of the family
\{\widetilde{C}_{\omega}(t)\}_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{I}}
, to show eq.(
5.9.3
) it is enough to show that for any
\eta\in(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*})^{I}
, we have

\dfrac{c_{\alpha}(\eta)}{|\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}=\widetilde{C}_{\alpha,\eta}(q).

By eq.(
5.1.4
), we deduce the following equality

\dfrac{c_{\alpha}(\eta)}{|\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}=\sum_{\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}}\langle c_{\alpha},\chi\rangle\dfrac{\chi(\eta^{-1})}{\chi(e)}\dfrac{\chi(e)}{|\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}

(5.9.4)

Rearranging the sum of the RHS of eq.(
5.9.4
) in terms of the multitypes of the characters, we have:

\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}\\
\chi\sim\omega\end{subarray}}\langle c_{\alpha},\chi\rangle\dfrac{\chi(\eta^{-1})}{\chi(e)}H_{\omega}^{\vee}(q)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\widetilde{C}_{\omega}(q)\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}\\
\chi\sim\omega\end{subarray}}\dfrac{\chi(\eta^{-1})}{\chi(e)}\right).

(5.9.5)

By Proposition
5.6.5
and the description of the irreducible characters of
\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
given in
section

5.6
, we can rewrite the RHS of eq.(
5.9.5
) as follows, using the notations of
section

5.8
:

\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\dfrac{\widetilde{C}_{\omega}(q)}{w(\omega)}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta:T_{S_{\omega}}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}\\
\Gamma_{\theta}=\Gamma_{S_{\omega}}\end{subarray}}\theta(\eta^{-1})\right)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}\dfrac{\widetilde{C}_{\omega}(q)}{w(\omega)}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\leq S_{\omega}\\
\text{ of level }\eta^{-1}\end{subarray}}P_{[S^{\prime}]}(q)\mu(S^{\prime},S_{\omega})\right)=\widetilde{C}_{\alpha,\eta}(q)

(5.9.6)

where the equality at the middle is a consequence of eq.(
5.8.3
) and the last equality is a consequence of the fact that
\mathcal{H}_{\eta,\alpha}=\mathcal{H}_{\eta^{-1},\alpha}
.

∎

Remark 5.9.4
.

For
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
and
\eta\in(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*})^{I}
, we say that
\eta
is
generic
with respect to
\beta
if
\mathcal{H}_{\eta,\beta}=\{\beta\}
.

For any
\beta
, if
q
is sufficienty big, there exists an element
\eta
of
(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*})^{I}
generic with respect to it. From eq.(
5.9.3
), we deduce that, if
\eta
is generic with respect to
\beta
, we have

\dfrac{c_{\beta}(\eta)}{|\Gl_{\beta}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}=\widetilde{C}_{\beta,gen}(q).

(5.9.7)

In particular, we remark that, for generic central elements, the quantity
\dfrac{c_{\beta}(\eta)}{|\Gl_{\beta}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}
is given by a rational function in
q
which does not depend on the choice of the generic
\eta
but only on the dimension vector
\beta
.

Fix now
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
. Assume that
q
is sufficiently big and for any
0<\beta\leq\alpha
, choose
\eta_{\beta,gen}\in(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*})^{I}
generic with respect to
\beta
.

Eq.(
5.9.3
) and eq.(
5.9.7
) give therefore a way to express the multiplicity
\dfrac{c_{\alpha}(\eta)}{|\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}
for any central element
\eta\in(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*})^{I}
in terms of the analogous values for the generic parameters
\eta_{\beta,gen}
.

The notion of dual Log compatibility is well behaved with respect to convolution, as explained by the following Lemma.

Lemma 5.9.5
.

Let
\{f_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}},\{f^{\prime}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
be two families of dual Log compatible class functions. The family
\{k_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
, defined as
k_{\alpha}\coloneqq\dfrac{f_{\alpha}\ast f^{\prime}_{\alpha}}{q^{\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}^{2}}}
is dual Log compatible.

Proof.

Let
F_{\omega}(t),F^{\prime}_{\omega,\alpha}(t)
be the polynomials such that
\langle f_{\alpha},\chi\rangle=F_{\omega}(q)
and
\langle f^{\prime}_{\alpha},\chi\rangle=F^{\prime}_{\omega}(q)
for every
\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
of multitype
\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
. By eq.(
5.1.3
), we see that

\langle k_{\alpha},\chi\rangle=\dfrac{F_{\omega}(q)F^{\prime}_{\omega}(q)}{H^{\vee}_{\omega}(q)q^{\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}^{2}}}.

Fix
d_{1},\dots,d_{r}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\omega_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{\beta_{1}},\dots,\omega_{r}\in\mathbb{T}_{\beta_{r}}
such that
\psi_{d_{1}}(\omega_{1})\ast\cdots\ast\psi_{d_{r}}(\omega_{r})=\omega.
To check eq.(
5.9.1
) for the functions
k_{\alpha}
, we need to verify that

\prod_{j=1}^{r}\dfrac{F_{\omega_{j}}(t^{d_{j}})F^{\prime}_{\omega_{j}}(t^{d_{j}})}{H^{\vee}_{\omega_{j}}(t^{d_{j}})t^{d_{j}\sum_{i\in I}(\beta_{j})_{i}^{2}}}\prod_{j=1}^{r}H^{\vee}_{\omega_{j}}(t^{d_{j}})=\dfrac{F_{\omega}(t)F^{\prime}_{\omega}(t)}{H^{\vee}_{\omega}(t)t^{\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}^{2}}}H^{\vee}_{\omega}(t).

(5.9.8)

Since the families
\{f_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}},\{f^{\prime}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
are dual Log compatible, this is equivalent to verify the equality

\left(\dfrac{\prod_{j=1}^{r}H^{\vee}_{\omega_{j}}(t^{d_{j}})}{H^{\vee}_{\omega}(t)}\right)^{2}=\dfrac{t^{\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}^{2}}}{\prod_{j=1}^{r}t^{\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}\sum_{i\in I}(\beta_{j})_{i}^{2}}}

(5.9.9)

which is a direct consequence of Identity (
5.7.1
).

∎

Remark 5.9.6
.

From Lemma
5.9.5
above and Example
5.9.2
, we deduce that for any
g\geq 1
, the family of class function
\{f^{g}_{n}:\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{C}\}
, where

f^{g}_{n}(h)\coloneqq\dfrac{\#\{(x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{g},y_{g})\ |\ \prod_{i=1}^{g}[x_{i},y_{i}]=h\}}{q^{(n^{2}(g-1))}}

is dual Log compatible.

In
[
30
, Section 2.3]
, the authors prove by a direct computation that
\langle f^{g}_{n},\chi\rangle=\left(\dfrac{|\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}{\chi(1)}\right)^{2g-1}
, from which it is possible to check dual Log compatibility directly from eq.(
5.9.1
).

6
Multiplicative quiver stacks for star-shaped quivers and character stacks for Riemann surfaces

In this chapter, we will apply the results of Theorem
5.9.3
to the count of points over finite fields of multiplicative quiver stacks and character stacks for Riemann surfaces. We start by recalling the definitions and the constructions of these objects.

6.1
Character stacks for Riemann surfaces

Fix integers
g,k\in\mathbb{N}
, a Riemann surface

\Sigma
of genus
g
and a divisor
D=\{d_{1},\dots,d_{k}\}\subseteq\Sigma
. In this paragraph we recall the definition of the character stacks for the Riemann surface
\Sigma
with fixed monodromies along
D
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k})
be a
k
-tuple of conjugacy classes of
\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
. Denote by
X_{\mathcal{C}}
the following variety

X_{\mathcal{C}}\coloneqq\{\rho\in\Hom(\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus D),\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C}))\ |\ \rho(\delta_{h})\in\overline{\mathcal{C}_{h}}\ \text{ for }h=1,\dots,k\}

where, for each
h=1,\dots,k
,
\delta_{h}
is a loop around the point
d_{h}
. The variety
X_{\mathcal{C}}
is the variety of representations of the fundamental group of
\Sigma\setminus D
with prescribed monodromy around the points of
D
lying in
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}},\dots,\overline{\mathcal{C}_{k}}
respectively.

Recall that the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus D)

admits the following explicit presentation

\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus D)=\langle a_{1},b_{1},\dots,a_{g},b_{g},\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{k}\ |\ [a_{1},b_{1}]\cdots[a_{g},b_{g}]\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{k}=1\rangle.

Therefore, the variety
X_{\mathcal{C}}
has the following explicit expression in terms of matrix equations:

X_{\mathcal{C}}=\Biggl\{(A_{1},B_{1},\dots,A_{g},B_{g},X_{1},\dots,X_{k})\in\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})^{2g}\times\overline{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{C}_{k}}\ |\ [A_{1},B_{1}]\cdots[A_{g},B_{g}]X_{1}\cdots X_{k}=1\Biggr\}.

The character stack
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}
associated to
(\Sigma,D,\mathcal{C})
is defined as the quotient stack

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\coloneqq[X_{\mathcal{C}}/\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})].

We also consider the character variety
M_{\mathcal{C}}
, defined as the GIT quotient

M_{\mathcal{C}}\coloneqq X_{\mathcal{C}}/\!\!/\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C}).

We will also consider certain quotient stacks, defined from Springer resolution of conjugacy classes, whose definition we briefly review here.

6.1.1
Springer resolutions of conjugacy classes

In this paragraph, the base field is
\mathbb{C}
. Consider a Levi subgroup
L\subseteq\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and a parabolic subgroup
P\supseteq L
having
L
as Levi factor. Let
U_{P}\subseteq P
be the unipotent radical. Fix an element
z\in Z_{L}
and let
Y_{z}
be the variety

Y_{z}\coloneqq\{(X,gP)\in\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})\times\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})/P\ |\ g^{-1}Xg\in zU_{P}\}.

Let
\pi_{z}:Y_{z}\to\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
be the projection
\pi_{z}((gP,X))=X
. The following proposition is well known (see for instance
[
23
]
and the reference therein for unipotent orbits).

Proposition 6.1.1
.

The image of
\pi_{z}
is the Zariski closure
\overline{C}
of a conjugacy class
C\subseteq\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and the morphism
\pi_{z}
is a resolution of singularities.

If
z\in(Z_{L})^{\reg}
, the map
\pi_{z}
is an isomorphism between
Y_{z}
and the conjugacy class of
z
in
\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.

The morphism
\pi_{z}:Y_{z}\to\overline{C}\subseteq\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is called a
partial Springer resolution
. Its image does not depend on the choice of the parabolic subgroup
P
.

Remark 6.1.2
.

The variety
Y_{z}
can be described in the following equivalent way. Consider
n_{0},\dots,n_{s}
such that
L\cong\Gl_{n_{0}}\times\cdots\times\Gl_{n_{s}}
. The element
z\in Z_{L}
corresponds therefore to
z_{0},\dots,z_{s}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that

z=(z_{0}I_{n_{0}},\dots,z_{s}I_{n_{s}}).

Let
P
be the parabolic subgroup containing
L
as a Levi factor and which contains the subgroup of upper triangular matrices. Identify
\Gl_{n}/P
with the corresponding partial flag variety in the classical way, i.e.

\Gl_{n}/P\cong\{\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{s}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{0}=\mathbb{C}^{n})\ |\ \dim(\mathcal{F}_{i})=\sum_{j=i}^{s}n_{j}\}.

(6.1.1)

We have

Y_{z}=\Bigg\{(X,\mathcal{F})\in\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})\times\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})/P\ |\ X(\mathcal{F}_{j})\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{j}\text{ for each $j=0,\dots,s$}

\text{ and the morphism induced by }X\text{ on }\mathcal{F}_{j}/\mathcal{F}_{j+1}\text{ is }z_{j}I_{n_{j}}\Bigg\}

Consider now a
k
-tuple of Levi subgroups
\mathbf{L}=(L_{1},\dots,L_{k})
, a
k
-tuple of parabolic subgroups
\mathbf{P}=(P_{1},\dots,P_{k})
where each
P_{i}
has
L_{i}
as Levi factor and of central elements
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})\in Z_{L_{1}}\times\cdots Z_{L_{k}}
.

Let now
X_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}
be the variety defined as

X_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}\coloneqq\Biggl\{(A_{1},B_{1},\dots,A_{g},B_{g},g_{1}P_{1},X_{1},\dots)\in\Gl_{n}^{2g}(\mathbb{C})\times\prod_{h=1}^{k}Y_{z_{h}}\ |\ \prod_{i=1}^{g}[A_{i},B_{i}]X_{1}\cdots X_{k}=1\Biggr\}

and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}
the quotient stack

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}\coloneqq[X_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}/\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})].

Let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k})
be the
k
-tuple such that
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{j}}
is the image of the projection
Y_{z_{j}}\to\Gl_{n}
.

Notice that the projections
\pi_{z_{1}},\dots,\pi_{z_{h}}
induce a morphism

\pi:X_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}\to X_{\mathcal{C}}

(A_{1},B_{1},\dots,A_{g},B_{g},g_{1}P_{1},X_{1},\dots,g_{k}P_{k},X_{k})\to(A_{1},B_{1},\dots,A_{g},B_{g},X_{1},\dots,X_{k}).

As
\pi
is
\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
-equivariant, it descends to a morphism of quotient stacks , which we still denote by
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Remark 6.1.3
.

Notice that, if
z_{1}\in(Z_{L_{1}})^{\reg},\dots,z_{k}\in(Z_{L_{k}})^{\reg}
, i.e. if each
\mathcal{C}_{h}
is semisimple, the morphism
\pi
is actually an isomorphism.

Remark 6.1.4
.

The morphism
\pi
is obtained by restricting the product of the partial Springer resolutions
Y_{z_{h}}\to\mathcal{C}_{h}
and then quotienting by
\Gl_{n}
. The decomposition theorem (and its equivariant version) for partial Springer resolutions are well understood in terms of the representation theory of Weyl groups.

Although we will not cover this in this article, it is natural to expect that the cohomological properties of the morphism
\pi
could have a similar description.

In what follows, we will show how to relate the stacks
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}
