to multiplicative quiver stacks for star-shaped quivers. We start by recalling some generalities about quivers and their multiplicative moment maps and fixing some notations.

6.2
Quiver representations

A quiver
Q
is an oriented graph
Q=(I,\Omega)
, where
I
is its set of vertices and
\Omega
is its set of arrows. We will always assume that
I,\Omega
are finite. For an arrow
a:i\to j
in
\Omega
we denote by
i=t(a)
its
tail
and by
j=h(a)
its
head
.

Fix a field
K
. A representation
M
of
Q
over
K
is given by a (finite dimensional)
K
-vector space
M_{i}
for each vertex
i\in I
and by linear maps
M_{a}:M_{t(a)}\to M_{h(a)}
for each
a\in\Omega
.

Given two representations
M,M^{\prime}
of
Q
, a morphism
f:M\to M^{\prime}
is given by maps
f_{i}:M_{i}\to M^{\prime}_{i}
such that, for all
a\in\Omega
, the following equality holds:
f_{h(a)}M_{a}=M^{\prime}_{a}f_{t(a)}
.
The category of representations of
Q
over
K
is denoted by
\Rep_{K}(Q)
. For a representation
M
, the
dimension vector

\dim M\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
is the vector
\dim M=(\dim M_{i})_{i\in I}
. It is an isomorphism invariant of the category
\Rep_{K}(Q)
.

For a representation
M
of dimension
\alpha
, up to fixing a basis of the vector spaces
M_{i}
for each
i\in I
, we can assume that
M_{i}=K^{\alpha_{i}}
. For
a\in\Omega
, the linear map
M_{a}:K^{t(a)}\to K^{h(a)}
can be therefore identified with a matrix in
\Mat(\alpha_{h(a)},\alpha_{t(a)},K)
.

Consider then the affine space

R(Q,\alpha)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{a\in\Omega}\Mat(\alpha_{h(a)},\alpha_{t(a)},K).

We can endow
R(Q,\alpha)
with the action of the group
\Gl_{\alpha}=\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I}\Gl_{\alpha_{i}}
defined as

g\cdot(M_{a})_{a\in\Omega}=(g_{h(a)}M_{a}g_{t(a)}^{-1})_{a\in\Omega}.

The orbits of this action are exactly the isomorphism classes of representations of
Q
of
\dim=\alpha
.

Finally, denote by
(-,-):\mathbb{Z}^{I}\times\mathbb{Z}^{I}\to\mathbb{Z}
the Euler form of
Q
, defined as

(\alpha,\beta)=\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}-\sum_{a\in\Omega}\alpha_{t(a)}\beta_{h(a)}.

6.3
Star-shaped quivers and multiplicative quiver stacks

Let
Q=(I,\Omega)
be the following star-shaped quiver with
g
loops on the central vertex
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ^{[1,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ^{[1,2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ^{[1,s_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ^{[2,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ^{[2,2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ^{[2,s_{2}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ^{[k,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ^{[k,2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ^{[k,s_{k}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

Let
(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{I}
be the subset of vectors with non-increasing coordinates along the legs and, more generally, for any subset
V\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{I}
, denote by
V^{*}\coloneqq V\cap(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
.

Denote by
\overline{Q}
the double quiver
\overline{Q}=(I,\overline{\Omega})
with the same set of vertices of
Q
and as set of arrows
\overline{\Omega}=\{a,a^{*}\ |\ a\in\Omega\}
, where
a^{*}:h(a)\to t(a)
.

For a representation
x\in R(Q,\alpha)
, for each
h=1,\dots,k
and
j=0,\dots,s_{h}
, we denote by
x_{h,j}\in\Mat(\alpha_{[h,j]},\alpha_{[h,j+1]},K)
the matrix associated to the arrow
a
having
t(a)=[h,j+1]
and
h(a)=[h,j]
, where we put
x_{h,s_{h}}=0
.

Similarly, for an element
\overline{x}\in R(\overline{Q},\alpha)
we denote by
x_{h,j}^{*}\in\Mat(\alpha_{[h,j+1]},\alpha_{[h,j]},K)
the matrix associated to the arrow
a^{*}\in\overline{\Omega}
.

Lastly, for a representation
\overline{x}\in R(\overline{Q},\alpha)
, we denote by
e_{1},\dots,e_{g},e_{1}^{*},\dots,e_{g}^{*}\in\Mat(\alpha_{0},K)
the matrix associated to the
g
loops of
Q
and the corresponding reversed arrows of
\overline{Q}
respectively.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
, let
R(\overline{Q},\alpha)^{\circ}\subseteq R(\overline{Q},\alpha)
be the open subset of representations
(x_{a},x_{a^{*}})_{a\in\Omega}
such that
(1+x_{a}x_{a^{*}}),(1+x_{a^{*}}x_{a})
is invertible for every
a\in\Omega
.

Let moreover
R(\overline{Q},\alpha)^{\circ,*}\subseteq R(\overline{Q},\alpha)^{\circ}
be the open subset of representations
(x_{a},x_{a^{*}})_{a\in\Omega}
such that
x_{a}
is injective for each
a\in\Omega
. Notice that
R(\overline{Q},\alpha)^{\circ,\ast}=\varnothing
if
\alpha\notin(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
.

Assume to have fixed an ordering
<
on
\Omega
. The multiplicative moment map
\Phi_{\alpha}^{*}
is the
\Gl_{\alpha}
-equivariant morphism

\Phi^{*}_{\alpha}:R(\overline{Q},\alpha)^{\circ,\ast}\to\Gl_{\alpha}

(x_{a},x_{a^{*}})\rightarrow\prod_{a\in\Omega}(1+x_{a}x_{a^{*}})(1+x_{a^{*}}x_{a})^{-1}

(6.3.1)

where we are taking the ordered product with respect to
<
.

For
\sigma\in(K^{*})^{I}
, the fiber
(\Phi^{*}_{\alpha})^{-1}(\sigma)
is
\Gl_{\alpha}
-stable and we define the multiplicative quiver stack
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\sigma,\alpha}
of parameter
\sigma,\alpha
as the quotient stack

\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\sigma,\alpha}\coloneqq[(\Phi^{*}_{\alpha})^{-1}(\sigma)/\Gl_{\alpha}].

Remark 6.3.1
.

The isomorphism class of the multiplicative quiver stack
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\sigma,\alpha}
does not depend on the ordering
<
of the arrows, see for example
[
10
, Theorem 1.4]
.

Example 6.3.2
.

Let
Q=(I,\Omega)
be the Jordan quiver, i.e. the quiver with one vertex and one arrow. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the variety
R(\overline{Q},n)
is
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(K)\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(K)
and the variety
R(\overline{Q},n)^{\circ,\ast}
is given by

R(\overline{Q},n)^{\circ,\ast}=\{(e,e^{*})\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(K)\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(K)\ |\ e,1+ee^{*},1+e^{*}e\in\Gl_{n}(K)\}.

Notice that the variety
R(\overline{Q},n)^{\circ,\ast}
is isomorphic to
\Gl_{n}(K)\times\Gl_{n}(K)
via the isomorphism

R(\overline{Q},n)^{\circ,\ast}\to\Gl_{n}(K)\times\Gl_{n}(K)

(e,e^{*})\to(e,e^{-1}+e^{*}).

Through this identification, the multiplicative moment map
\Phi^{\ast}_{n}
corresponds to the morphism

\Phi_{n}^{\ast}:\Gl_{n}(K)\times\Gl_{n}(K)\to\Gl_{n}(K)

\Phi^{\ast}_{n}(A,B)=[A,B].

For a point
\overline{x}\in(\Phi_{\alpha}^{*})^{-1}(\sigma)
, we have the following relationships. At the central vertex, we have:

\prod_{l=1}^{g}(1+e_{l}e_{l}^{*})(1+e_{l}^{*}e_{l})^{-1}\prod_{h=1}^{k}(1+x_{h,0}x_{h,0}^{*})=\sigma_{0}I_{\alpha_{0}}

(6.3.2)

For any
h=1,\dots,k
and
j=1,\dots,s_{h}
, we have

(1+x_{h,j}x^{*}_{h,j})(1+x_{h,j-1}^{*}x_{h,j-1})^{-1}=\sigma_{[h,j]}I_{\alpha_{[h,j]}}

(6.3.3)

which can be rewritten as

x_{h,j}x^{*}_{h,j}-\sigma_{[h,j]}x_{h,j-1}^{*}x_{h,j-1}=(\sigma_{[h,j]}-1)I_{\alpha_{[h,j]}}.

(6.3.4)

For
j=s_{h}
, we have

\sigma_{[h,s_{h}]}x_{h,s_{h}-1}^{*}x_{h,s_{h}-1}=(1-\sigma_{[h,s_{h}]})I_{\alpha_{[h,s_{h}]}}.

(6.3.5)

6.4
Multiplicative quiver stacks and character stacks: the main isomorphism

Consider a
k
-tuple of Levi subgroups
\mathbf{L}=(L_{1},\dots,L_{k})
, a
k
-tuple of parabolic subgroups
\mathbf{P}=(P_{1},\dots,P_{k})
such that
P_{h}
has
L_{h}
as Levi factor for every
h
and a
k
-tuple of central elements
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})\in Z_{L_{1}}\times\cdots\times Z_{L_{k}}
, as in
section

6.1.1
.

Assume that, for each
h=1,\dots,k
,
L_{h}
is the Levi subgroup of
\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})

L_{h}=\prod_{j=0}^{s_{h}}\Gl_{n_{h,j}}(\mathbb{C})

and

z_{h}=(z_{h,0}I_{n_{h,0}},\dots,z_{h,s_{h}}I_{n_{h,s_{h}}})

with
z_{h,0},\dots,z_{h,s_{h}}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

Consider now a star-shaped quiver
Q=(I,\Omega)
with
g
loops on the central vertex and
k
legs of length
s_{1},\dots,s_{k}
respectively. Define the following dimension vector
\alpha_{\mathbf{L}}\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
. For each
h=1,\dots,k
and
j=0,\dots,s_{h}
, put

\displaystyle(\alpha_{\mathbf{L}})_{[h,j]}\coloneqq\sum_{l=j}^{s_{h}}n_{h,j}

(6.4.1)

where we are identifying
[h,0]=0
. Define the following element
\sigma_{\mathbf{z}}\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{I}
.

\begin{cases}(\sigma_{\mathbf{z}})_{0}\coloneqq\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{k}z^{-1}_{j,0}\\
(\sigma_{\mathbf{z}})_{[h,j]}\coloneqq z_{h,j-1}z_{h,j}^{-1}\text{ if }j\geq 1.\end{cases}

We have the following result, relating multiplicative quiver stacks for star-shaped quivers and Springer resolutions of conjugacy classes.

Theorem 6.4.1
.

For any
\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}
as above, there is an isomorphism of stacks

\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\alpha_{\mathbf{L}},\sigma_{\mathbf{z}}}\cong\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}.

In the proof, we suppose
g=0
and we put
\sigma=\sigma_{\mathbf{z}},\alpha=\alpha_{\mathbf{L}}
to simplify the notations. The case of
g>0
is a combination of the arguments used in this proof and that of
[
9
, Proposition 2]
.

Proof.

We define the following morphism

f:(\Phi^{*}_{\alpha})^{-1}(\sigma)\to X_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}.

For an element
\overline{x}\in(\Phi^{*}_{\alpha})^{-1}(\sigma)
, consider the flag

\mathcal{F}_{j,\overline{x}}=(\mathbb{C}^{n}\supseteq\Imm(x_{j,0})\supseteq\Imm(x_{j,0}x_{j,1})\supseteq\cdots\supseteq\Imm(x_{j,0}\cdots x_{j,s_{j}-1})).

Notice that, for each
h=0,\dots,s_{j}-1
, we have

\dim(\Imm(x_{j,0}\cdots x_{j,h}))=\alpha_{[j,h+1]},

since
x_{j,r}
is injective for each
j
and
r
. In particular,
\mathcal{F}_{j,\overline{x}}
belongs to the partial flag variety
\Gl_{n}/P_{j}
. We define therefore

f(\overline{x})=(\mathcal{F}_{1,\overline{x}},z_{1,0}+z_{1,0}x_{1,0}x_{1,0}^{*},\mathcal{F}_{2,\overline{x}},\dots,z_{k,0}+z_{k,0}x_{k,0}x_{k,0}^{*}).

For each
h=1,\dots,k
, put
X_{h}\coloneqq z_{h,0}+z_{h,0}x_{h,0}x_{h,0}^{*}
. Notice that from eq.(
6.3.2
), we have that

X_{1}\cdots X_{k}=1.

To check that the morphism
f
is well defined we need to check thus the following two conditions.

1.

The flag
\mathcal{F}_{h,\overline{x}}
is
X_{h}
invariant for each
h
.

2.

The morphism that
X_{h}
induces on the quotient space

\Imm(x_{h,0}\cdots x_{h,j-1})/\Imm(x_{h,0}\cdots x_{h,j})

and which we denote by
\overline{X}_{h,j}
is equal to
z_{h,j}I_{n_{h,j}}
. Here we are putting
\Imm(x_{h,-1})=\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

From eq.(
6.3.4
), by recurrence, we deduce that for each
h=1,\dots,k
and each
j=0,\dots,s_{h}-1
and
v\in\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{[h,j]}}
, we have

X_{j}(x_{h,0}\cdots x_{h,j-1}(v))=z_{h,0}x_{h,0}\cdots x_{h,j}(v)+z_{h,0}x_{h,0}x_{h,0}^{*}x_{h,0}\cdots x_{h,j}(v)=

(6.4.2)

=\dfrac{z_{h,0}}{\sigma_{[h,1]}\cdots\sigma_{[h,j]}}x_{h,0}\cdots x_{h,j-1}(v)+x_{h,0}\cdots x_{h,j}x_{h,j}x_{h,j}^{*}(v)

(6.4.3)

where we are putting
x_{h,s_{h}}=x_{h,s_{h}}^{*}=0
.

Notice that

\dfrac{z_{h,0}}{\sigma_{[h,1]}\cdots\sigma_{[h,j]}}=z_{h,j}.

From eq.(
6.4.3
), we deduce therefore that properties
1),2)
above are respected for each
h,j
and therefore
f
is well defined.

Denote by
I^{\prime}=I\setminus\{0\}
and put
\displaystyle\Gl_{\alpha}^{\prime}\coloneqq\prod_{i\in I^{\prime}}\Gl_{\alpha_{i}}
. We have thus that

\Gl_{\alpha}=\Gl_{n}\times\Gl_{\alpha}^{\prime}.

(6.4.4)

It can be verified that the action of
\Gl_{\alpha}^{\prime}
on
(\Phi_{\alpha}^{*})^{-1}(\sigma)
is schematically free and therefore the multiplicative quiver stack
[(\Phi_{\alpha}^{*})^{-1}(\sigma)/\Gl^{\prime}_{\alpha}]
is actually an algebraic variety.

In addition, notice that the map
f
is
\Gl_{\alpha}^{\prime}
-invariant. Denote by

\widetilde{f}:(\Phi_{\alpha}^{*})^{-1}(\sigma)/\Gl_{\alpha}^{\prime}\to X_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}

the associated morphism. From the identity (
6.4.4
), to show that

\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\alpha,\sigma}\cong\mathcal{M}_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}},

it is sufficient to show that
\widetilde{f}
is an isomorphism.

We define the following morphism
\theta:X_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}\to(\Phi_{\alpha}^{*})^{-1}(\sigma)/\Gl_{\alpha}^{\prime}
. Consider an element

(\mathcal{F}_{1},X_{1},\dots,\mathcal{F}_{k},X_{k})\in X_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}.

For each
h=1,\dots,k
and
j=1,\dots,s_{h}
, fix a basis of the vector space
\mathcal{F}_{h,j}
and denote by

x_{h,j-1}:\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{[h,j]}}\to\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{[h,j-1]}}

the matrix such that
z_{h,j-1}x_{h,j-1}
is the matrix of the inclusion
\mathcal{F}_{h,j}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{h,j-1}
in the respective fixed basis.

By definition of
X_{\mathbf{L},\mathbf{P},\mathbf{z}}
, we have that

(X_{h}-z_{h,j}I_{n})(\mathcal{F}_{h,j})\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{h,j+1},

i.e.
X_{h}-z_{h,j}I_{n}
defines a morphism
\mathcal{F}_{h,j}\to\mathcal{F}_{h,j+1}
and we denote by

x_{h,j}^{*}:\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{[h,j]}}\to\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{[h,j+1]}}

its associated matrix in the fixed basis.

By definition, for each
h=1,\dots,k
, we have

X_{h}=z_{h,0}+z_{h,0}x_{h,0}x_{h,0}^{*}

(6.4.5)

Moreover, for each
j=1,\dots,s_{h}
, we have that
z_{h,j}x_{j,h}x_{h,j}^{*}
is the matrix associated to the morphism

X_{j}-z_{h,j}I_{n}:\mathcal{F}_{h,j}\to\mathcal{F}_{h,j}

and
x_{h,j-1}^{*}z_{h,j-1}x_{h,j-1}
is the matrix associated to the morphism

X_{j}-z_{h,j-1}I_{n}:\mathcal{F}_{h,j}\to\mathcal{F}_{h,j}

in the respective basis.

In particular, we have that

z_{h,j}x_{j,h}x_{h,j}^{*}-z_{h,j-1}x_{h,j-1}^{*}x_{h,j-1}=(z_{h,j}-z_{h,j-1})I_{\alpha_{[h,j]}}

(6.4.6)

and, since
\dfrac{z_{h,j-1}}{z_{h,j}}=\sigma_{[h,j]}
, we find

x_{j,h}x_{h,j}^{*}-\sigma_{[h,j]}x_{h,j-1}^{*}x_{h,j-1}=(1-\sigma_{[h,j]})I_{\alpha_{[h,j]}}

(6.4.7)

By eq.(
6.3.4
), we deduce that
(x_{h,j},x_{h,j}^{*})_{\begin{subarray}{c}h=1,\dots,k\\
j=0,\dots,s_{h}-1\end{subarray}}
defines a point
\overline{x}\in(\Phi_{\alpha}^{*})^{-1}(\sigma)
and we put

\theta(\mathcal{F}_{1},X_{1},\dots,\mathcal{F}_{k},X_{k})=\overline{x}.

From eq.(
6.4.5
) and the definition of
\overline{x}
, we deduce that
\theta
and
\widetilde{f}
are inverse to one aother, i.e. that
\widetilde{f}
is an isomorphism.
∎

Consider now a
k
-tuple of semisimple conjugacy classes
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{k})
such that each
\mathcal{C}_{h}
is semisimple (i.e.

\overline{\mathcal{C}_{h}}=\mathcal{C}_{h}
) and it is the conjugacy class of a diagonal matrix
C_{h}
with distinct eigenvalues
\gamma_{h,0},\dots,\gamma_{h,s_{h}}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
and multiplicities
n_{h,0},\dots,n_{h,s_{h}}
respectively.

Let
Q=(I,\Omega)
be the star-shaped quiver introduced in
section

6.3
, with
g
loops on the central vertex and
k
legs of length
s_{1},\dots,s_{k}
respectively.

Let
\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
be the dimension vector defined as

(\alpha_{\mathcal{C}})_{[h,j]}=\sum_{l=j}^{s_{h}}n_{h,l}

and
\gamma_{\mathcal{C}}\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{I}
the element defined as

\displaystyle(\gamma_{\mathcal{C}})_{[i,j]}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{k}\gamma^{-1}_{i,0}\text{ if }j=0\\
\gamma^{-1}_{i,j}\gamma_{i,j-1}\text{ otherwise }\par\end{cases}.

From Remark
6.1.3
and Theorem
6.4.1
, we deduce the following Theorem:

Theorem 6.4.2
.

For any
k
-tuple of semisimple conjugacy classes
\mathcal{C}
, there is an isomorphism of stacks

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\cong\mathcal{M}^{\ast}_{\gamma_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}.

Remark 6.4.3
.

The relation between multiplicative moment map for star-shaped quivers and character stacks and varieties was first introduced in the articles
[
9
, Proposition 2]
,
[
10
, Lemma 8.2]
.

The analogous of Theorem
6.4.2
for the corresponding GIT quotients, i.e. that
M_{\mathcal{C}}\cong M^{*}_{\gamma_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}
has been shown in
[
57
, Proposition 4.1]
. In
[
57
, Proposition 4.1]
, the author actually shows a stronger statement. In particular, the author does not consider only
k
-tuples of semisimple conjugacy classes.

In this case, it is necessary to slightly modify the definition of the multiplicative moment map and define it as a map

\Phi^{l}_{\beta}:R(\overline{Q},\alpha)^{\circ}\to\Gl_{\alpha},

i.e. we are not asking that all the maps
x_{h,j}
are injective. The proof of
[
57
, Proposition 4.1]
is quite similar to the one given for Theorem
6.4.1
.

The main difference is that, in the case of GIT quotients, in the part of the proof of Theorem
6.4.1
where we use that we restricted to
R(\overline{Q},\alpha)^{\circ,*}
, i.e. that the action of
\Gl_{\alpha}^{\prime}
is free, Daisuke instead uses the fundamental theorem of invariant theory. This latter type of technique has already been introduced by
[
37
]
.

6.5
Dual Log compatibility for moment map

In this chapter, we show how to relate the results about dual Log compatible families of
section

5.9
to the study of multiplicative quiver stacks for star-shaped quivers.

Consider now the construction of
section

6.3
in the case in which
K=\mathbb{F}_{q}
. We denote by
m_{\alpha}:\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{C}
the class function defined as

m_{\alpha}(g)\coloneqq\dfrac{|(\Phi^{*}_{\alpha})^{-1}(g)^{F}|}{q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}.

Notice that, for
\sigma\in(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*})^{I}
, we have an equality

\dfrac{m_{\alpha}(\sigma)}{|\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}=\dfrac{\#\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\sigma,\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}{q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}.

For the family of class functions
\{m_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
we have the following Theorem:

Theorem 6.5.1
.

The family
\{m_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
is dual Log compatible

The proof of Theorem
6.5.1
will be given in paragraph
section

6.6
below. For the consequences of Theorem
6.5.1
on the computation of the cohomology of character stacks of Riemann surfaces over
\mathbb{C}
see
section

7
.

6.6
Proof of Theorem
6.5.1

Theorem
6.5.1
will be proved through several steps.

We start by showing Theorem
6.5.1
in the case where
Q=(I,\Omega)
is the star-shaped quiver with two vertices
I=\{0,1\}
and one arrow
a:1\to 0
between them (i.e.
g=0
and
k=1
). This is usually called the Kronecker quiver.

Remark 6.6.1
.

The proof of the case of the Kronecker quiver (i.e. of Lemma
6.6.3
below) is the main technical point of the proof of Theorem
6.5.1
. The proof of this Lemma involves computations in the character ring of
\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

In particular, we will have to understand combinatorially multiplicities of the type
\langle\chi_{0},R^{G}_{L}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2})\rangle
where
\chi_{0}\in\Gl_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
,
L=\Gl_{n}\times\Gl_{n^{\prime}}
with
n+n^{\prime}=m
and
\chi_{1}\in\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee},\chi_{2}\in\Gl_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
.

We will mainly need two results about representations of finite reductive groups that we recalled before.

•

Proposition
5.6.4
regarding the decomposition into irreducible
\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-representations of the Deligne-Lusztig induction of an irreducible
L^{F}
-representation, with
L\subseteq\Gl_{n}
an
F
-stable Levi subgroup.

•

The description of certain connected centralizers in the case of
\Gl_{n}
given in
section

5.5.2
.

We show how to extend the result from the case of the Kronecker quiver to any star-shaped quiver in Proof
6.6
.

For the Kronecker quiver
Q
, a dimension vector
\alpha
is thus a couple
\alpha=(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
and the function
m_{\alpha}(g_{0},g_{1})
for a couple
(g_{0},g_{1})\in\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\times\Gl_{\alpha_{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is given by

m_{\alpha}(g_{0},g_{1})=\dfrac{\#\{f\in\Hom^{inj}(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}}),f^{*}\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}})\ |\ 1+ff^{*}=g_{0},\ 1+f^{*}f=g_{1}^{-1}\}}{q^{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{0}^{2}-\alpha_{1}^{2}}}.

Remark 6.6.2
.

Notice that given
f\in\Hom^{inj}(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}})
and
f^{*}\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}})
such that
1+ff^{*}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, then
1+f^{*}f
is invertible too. It is enough to check that
1+f^{*}f
is injective. Given
x,y\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}}
such that
(1+f^{*}f)(x)=(1+f^{*}f)(y)
we have indeed

f\circ(1+f^{*}f)(x)=f\circ(1+f^{*}f)(y)

and, given that
f\circ(1+f^{*}f)=(1+ff^{*})\circ f
and
1+ff^{*}
is invertible, we deduce that
f(x)=f(y)
and so that
x=y
.

Lemma 6.6.3
.

In the case in which
Q
is the Kronecker quiver, the family
\{m_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
is dual Log compatible.

Proof.

We have that
m_{\alpha}\equiv 0
if
\alpha\not\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
. Fix then
\alpha\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
and denote by
\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1}
. Fix an irreducible character
\chi=\chi_{0}\boxtimes\chi_{1}\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
with
\chi_{i}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{i}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
for
i=0,1
. We have:

\langle m_{\alpha},\chi\rangle=\dfrac{1}{|\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in\Hom^{inj}(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}})\\
f^{*}\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}})\\
\text{ s.t }1+ff^{*}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\end{subarray}}\chi_{0}(1+ff^{*})\chi_{1}((1+f^{*}f)^{-1}).

(6.6.1)

I Step: rewriting the RHS of eq.(
6.6.1
)

Let
J_{\alpha}\in\Hom^{inj}(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}})
be the block matrix given by the identity on the first
\alpha_{1}
rows and
0
everywhere else and let
P_{\alpha}
be the stabiliser of
J_{\alpha}
inside
\Gl_{\alpha}
.

Notice that
P_{\alpha}
is isomorphic to the parabolic subgroup
P\subseteq\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}
, given by the matrices which preserve the image of
J_{\alpha}
.

We denote by
L\subseteq P
the Levi subgroup given by
\Gl_{\alpha_{1}}\times\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}
embedded block diagonally.

The action of
\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
on
\Hom^{inj}(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}})
is transitive and we can therefore identity the latter set with
\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})/P_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
via the map which sends
(g_{0},g_{1})P_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to g_{0}J_{\alpha}g_{1}^{-1}
.

We can thus rewrite the sum above as :

\dfrac{1}{|\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}\sum_{(g_{0},g_{1})P_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})/P_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}

(6.6.2)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f^{*}\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}})\\
\text{ s.t. }1+g_{0}J_{\alpha}g_{1}^{-1}f^{*}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\end{subarray}}\chi_{0}(1+g_{0}J_{\alpha}g_{1}^{-1}f^{*})\chi_{1}((1+f^{*}g_{0}J_{\alpha}g_{1}^{-1})^{-1}).

(6.6.3)

For each
(g_{0},g_{1})P_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
we can rewrite the last term of eq.(
6.6.3
) as

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f^{*}\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}})\\
\text{ s.t. }1+J_{\alpha}g_{1}^{-1}f^{*}g_{0}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\end{subarray}}\chi_{0}(g_{0}(1+J_{\alpha}g_{1}^{-1}f^{*}g_{0})g_{0}^{-1})\chi_{1}(g_{1}(1+g_{1}^{-1}f^{*}g_{0}J_{\alpha})^{-1}g_{1}^{-1}).

(6.6.4)

As
\chi_{0},\chi_{1}
are class functions, we can rewrite the sum in eq.(
6.6.4
) as

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f^{*}\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}})\\
\text{ s.t. }1+J_{\alpha}g_{1}^{-1}f^{*}g_{0}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\end{subarray}}\chi_{0}(1+J_{\alpha}g_{1}^{-1}f^{*}g_{0})\chi_{1}((1+g_{1}^{-1}f^{*}g_{0}J_{\alpha})^{-1}).

(6.6.5)

Moreover, for each
(g_{0},g_{1})\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we have a bijection

\{f^{*}\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}})\ |\ 1+J_{\alpha}f^{*}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}\}\leftrightarrow\{f^{*}\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}})\ |\ 1+J_{\alpha}g_{1}^{-1}f^{*}g_{0}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}\}

f^{*}\to g_{1}f^{*}g_{0}^{-1}.

and so

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f^{*}\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}})\\
\text{ s.t. }1+J_{\alpha}g_{1}^{-1}f^{*}g_{0}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\end{subarray}}\chi_{0}(1+J_{\alpha}g_{1}^{-1}f^{*}g_{0})\chi_{1}((1+g_{1}^{-1}f^{*}g_{0}J_{\alpha})^{-1})=

(6.6.6)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f^{*}\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}})\\
\text{ s.t. }1+J_{\alpha}f^{*}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\end{subarray}}\chi_{0}(1+J_{\alpha}f^{*})\chi_{1}((1+f^{*}J_{\alpha})^{-1}).

From eq.(
6.6.6
), we deduce that the sum in eq.(
6.6.2
) can be rewritten as

\dfrac{1}{|P_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f^{*}\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{0}},\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\alpha_{1}})\\
\text{ s.t. }1+J_{\alpha}f^{*}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\end{subarray}}\chi_{0}(1+J_{\alpha}f^{*})\chi_{1}((1+f^{*}J_{\alpha})^{-1})

(6.6.7)

Writing
f^{*}
as a block matrix
(A|B)
with
A\in\Mat(\alpha_{1},\mathbb{F}_{q})
and
B\in\Mat(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1},\mathbb{F}_{q})
, where
\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1}
, we have

1+J_{\alpha}f^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}1+A&B\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

and
1+f^{*}J_{\alpha}=1+A
. We can rewrite the sum of eq.(
6.6.7
) as

\dfrac{1}{|P_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\Gl_{\alpha_{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\\
B\in\Mat(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1},\mathbb{F}_{q})\end{subarray}}\chi_{0}\left(\begin{pmatrix}M&B\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\right)\chi_{1}(M^{-1})

(6.6.8)

Moreover, for any
M\in\Gl_{\alpha_{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we have that
\chi_{1}(M^{-1})=\chi_{1}^{*}(M)
and we can thus rewrite the sum in eq.(
6.6.8
) as

\dfrac{1}{|P_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\Gl_{\alpha_{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\\
B\in\Mat(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1},\mathbb{F}_{q})\end{subarray}}\chi_{0}\left(\begin{pmatrix}M&B\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\right)\chi^{*}_{1}(M)

(6.6.9)

II Step: relate the sum in eq.(
6.6.9
) to computations in the character ring of
\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})

Consider now the class function
H\in\mathcal{C}(\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\times\Gl_{\alpha_{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
defined as

H\coloneqq\sum_{\chi_{2}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}}(\chi_{2}\boxtimes\chi^{*}_{1})\dfrac{\chi_{2}(1)}{\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}.

By eq.(
5.1.1
), for any
(N,M)\in\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\times\Gl_{\alpha_{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
we have that

H((N,M))=\begin{cases}0\text{ if }N\neq 1\\
\chi_{1}^{*}(M)\text{ otherwise }.\end{cases}

We deduce that the sum in eq.(
6.6.9
) can be rewritten as

\dfrac{1}{|P(\mathbb{F}_{q})|q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}\sum_{\chi_{2}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}}\sum_{h\in P(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\chi_{0}(h)\Infl_{L}^{P}(\chi^{*}_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2})(h)\dfrac{\chi_{2}(1)}{|\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|},

(6.6.10)

where we are using that
|P(\mathbb{F}_{q})|=|P_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|
. Denote by
\Res_{P}:\mathcal{C}(\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\to\mathcal{C}(P(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
the restriction of class functions. We have therefore that the quantity in eq.(
6.6.10
) is equal to

=\dfrac{1}{q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}\sum_{\chi_{2}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}}\langle\Res_{P}(\chi_{0}),\Infl_{L}^{P}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2})\rangle\dfrac{\chi_{2}(1)}{|\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}

(6.6.11)

which by Frobenius reciprocity is equal to

=\dfrac{1}{q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}\sum_{\chi_{2}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}}\langle\chi_{0},R^{G}_{L}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2})\rangle\dfrac{\chi_{2}(1)}{|\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}.

(6.6.12)

III Step: understand the RHS of eq.(
6.6.12
) for characters of unipotent type

Suppose now that the type of
\chi=\chi_{0}\boxtimes\chi_{1}
is
(1,\bm{\lambda})
, where
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda^{0},\lambda^{1})
with
\lambda^{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{0}},\lambda^{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{1}}
. We have then
\chi_{0}=(\gamma\circ\det)R_{\lambda^{0}}
and
\chi_{1}=(\gamma\circ\det)R_{\lambda^{1}}
, with
\gamma\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*},\mathbb{C}^{*})
.

Let
\chi_{2}=\epsilon_{\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}}\epsilon_{L_{2}}R^{\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}}_{L_{2}}(\theta_{2}R_{\tilde{\varphi_{2}}})
for a certain
\varphi_{2}\in(W_{L_{2}})^{F}
and a certain reduced
\theta_{2}:L_{2}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
. From Lemma
5.2.3
, we have an equality

R^{G}_{L}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2})=\epsilon_{\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}}\epsilon_{L_{2}}R^{G}_{\Gl_{\alpha_{1}}\times L_{2}}(((\gamma\circ\det)\times\theta_{2})(R_{\lambda^{1}}\boxtimes R_{\tilde{\varphi_{2}}})).

Let
L^{\prime}
be the connected centralizer of
(\gamma\circ\det)\times\theta_{2}:\Gl_{\alpha_{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\times L^{F}_{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
. By Remark
5.6.4
, the character
R^{G}_{L}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2})
belongs to the vector space spanned by irreducible characters with semisimple part
(L^{\prime},(\gamma\circ\det)\times\theta_{2})
.

The multiplicity
\langle(\gamma\circ\det)R_{\lambda_{0}},R^{G}_{L}((\gamma\circ\det)R_{\lambda^{1}}\boxtimes\chi_{2})\rangle
is therefore equal to
0
if
L^{\prime}
is different from
\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}
. Since
\theta_{2}:L_{2}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
is reduced, from the remarks made in
section

5.5.2
, we deduce that if
L^{\prime}=\Gl_{\alpha_{0}}
, we must have that
L_{2}=\Gl_{\alpha_{2}}
and that
\chi_{2}
is given by
(\gamma\circ\det)R_{\lambda^{2}}
for
\lambda^{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{2}}
.

From Remark
5.2.4
, the RHS of eq. (
6.6.11
) is thus equal to:

\dfrac{1}{q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}\sum_{\lambda^{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{2}}}\langle(\gamma\circ\det)R_{\lambda^{0}},(\gamma\circ\det)R^{G}_{L}(R_{\lambda^{1}}\boxtimes R_{\lambda^{2}})\rangle\dfrac{(-1)^{\alpha_{2}}}{q^{\frac{\alpha_{2}(\alpha_{2}-1)}{2}-n(\lambda^{2})}H_{\lambda^{2}}(q)}.

(6.6.13)

From eq.(
5.3.2
) , the sum in eq.(
6.6.13
) is equal to

\dfrac{1}{q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}\sum_{\lambda^{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{2}}}\langle\chi_{\lambda^{0}},\Ind_{S_{\alpha_{1}}\times S_{\alpha_{2}}}^{S_{\alpha_{0}}}(\chi_{\lambda^{1}}\boxtimes\chi_{\lambda^{2}})\rangle\dfrac{(-1)^{\alpha_{2}}}{q^{\frac{\alpha_{2}(\alpha_{2}-1)}{2}-n(\lambda^{2})}H_{\lambda^{2}}(q)}=

(6.6.14)

=\dfrac{1}{q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}\sum_{\lambda^{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{2}}}c_{\lambda^{1},\lambda^{2}}^{\lambda^{0}}\dfrac{(-1)^{\alpha_{2}}}{q^{\frac{\alpha_{2}(\alpha_{2}-1)}{2}-n(\lambda^{2})}H_{\lambda^{2}}(q)}

(6.6.15)

For any couple of partitions
(\lambda,\mu)
denote by
C_{\lambda,\mu}(t)\in{\mathbb{Q}}(t)
the function defined as

C_{\lambda,\mu}(t)=\begin{cases}0\text{ if }|\lambda|<|\mu|\\
\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{t^{|\lambda||\mu|-|\lambda|^{2}-|\mu|^{2}}}\sum_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{|\lambda|-|\mu|}}c^{\lambda}_{\mu,\nu}\dfrac{(-1)^{|\lambda|-|\mu|}}{t^{\frac{|\nu|(|\nu|-1)}{2}-n(\nu)}H_{\nu}(t)}.\end{cases}.

The reasoning above shows that for any
\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
of type
(1,\bm{\lambda})
, there is an equality

\langle m_{\alpha},\chi\rangle=C_{\lambda^{0},\lambda^{1}}(q).

IV Step: understand the RHS of eq.(
6.6.12
) for characters of any type

Let now
\delta\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
and consider
\chi=\chi_{0}\boxtimes\chi_{1}\in\Gl_{\delta}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
of multitype
\omega\in\mathbb{T}_{\delta}
, where
\omega=(d_{1},\bm{\lambda}_{1})\cdots(d_{r},\bm{\lambda}_{r})
, where for
j=1,\dots,r
we have
\bm{\lambda}_{j}=(\lambda_{j}^{0},\lambda_{j}^{1})\in\mathcal{P}^{2}
and we put
\beta_{j}=|\bm{\lambda}_{j}|
. Consider the Levi subgroups
\displaystyle L_{0}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}(\Gl_{(\beta_{j})_{0}})_{d_{j}}
and
\displaystyle L_{1}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}(\Gl_{(\beta_{j})_{1}})_{d_{j}}
.

There exist reduced characters
\theta^{0}:L_{0}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
and
\theta^{1}:L_{1}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
such that

\chi_{0}=R^{G}_{L_{0}}(\theta^{0}R_{\lambda_{1}^{0}}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes R_{\lambda_{r}^{0}})

and

\chi_{1}=R^{\Gl_{\delta_{1}}}_{L_{1}}(\theta^{1}R_{\lambda_{1}^{1}}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes R_{\lambda_{r}^{1}})

and
\theta^{0},\theta^{1}
are associated to the same
r
-tuple
(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{r})\in\Hom(\mathbb{F}_{q^{d_{1}}}^{*},\mathbb{C}^{*})\times\cdots\times\Hom(\mathbb{F}^{*}_{q^{d_{r}}},\mathbb{C}^{*})
, via
the correspondence of
section

5.5.2
. We denote by
\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{0}},\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{1}}\in\mathcal{P}^{r}
the multipartitions
\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{0}}=(\lambda_{1}^{0},\dots,\lambda_{r}^{0}),

\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{1}}=(\lambda_{1}^{1},\dots,\lambda_{r}^{1})
.

To verify the dual Log compatibility of the family
\{m_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
, it is enough to check that we have:

\langle m_{\delta},\chi\rangle H^{\vee}_{\omega}(q)=\prod_{j=1}^{r}C_{\lambda_{j}^{0},\lambda_{j}^{1}}(q^{d_{j}})H^{\vee}_{(1,\bm{\lambda}_{j})}(q^{d_{j}})

(6.6.16)

Notice that, if
\delta\not\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
, there must exist
\beta_{j}
such that
\beta_{j}\not\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
, i.e. such that
\lambda_{j}^{0}<\lambda_{j}^{1}
. Eq.(
6.6.16
) therefore is true as both sides are equal to
0
.

Assume then that
\delta\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
. From eq.(
6.6.11
), there is an equality

\langle m_{\delta},\chi\rangle=\dfrac{1}{q^{-(\delta,\delta)}}\sum_{\chi_{2}\in\Gl_{\delta_{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}}\langle\chi_{0},R^{G}_{M}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2})\rangle\dfrac{\chi_{2}(1)}{|\Gl_{\delta_{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}

(6.6.17)

where
M=\Gl_{\delta_{1}}\times\Gl_{\delta_{2}}\subseteq G
. Let
\chi_{2}=\epsilon_{\Gl_{\delta_{2}}}\epsilon_{L_{2}}R^{G}_{L_{2}}(\theta^{2}R_{\tilde{\varphi_{2}}})
, with
L_{2}\subseteq\Gl_{\delta_{2}}
a Levi subgroup and
\theta^{2}:L_{2}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
a reduced character. From Lemma
5.2.3
, there is an equality

R^{G}_{M}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2})=R^{G}_{L_{1}\times L_{2}}((\theta^{1}\times\theta^{2})(R_{\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{1}}}\boxtimes R_{\tilde{\varphi}_{2}})).

Let
L^{\prime}
be the connected centralizer of
\theta^{1}\times\theta^{2}:L_{1}\times L_{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
. From Proposition
5.6.4
, we have that
\langle\chi_{0},R^{G}_{M}(\chi_{1}\boxtimes\chi_{2})\rangle=0
if the semisimple part
(L^{\prime},\theta^{1}\times\theta^{2})
is not conjugated to
(L_{0},\theta^{0})
.

From the discussion made in
section

5.5.2
, we deduce that, if
(L^{\prime},\theta^{1}\times\theta^{2})
is conjugated to
(L^{0},\theta^{0})
, we must have that
|\lambda_{j}^{0}|\geq|\lambda_{j}^{1}|
, i.e.
\beta_{j}\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
, for each
j=1,\dots,r
.

If there exists
j\in\{1,\dots,r\}
such that
\beta_{j}\not\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
, eq.(
6.6.16
) is thus verified as both sides are equal to
0
.

If
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{r}\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
, from the remarks made in
5.5.2
, we deduce that there exist a unique couple
(L_{2},\theta^{2})
, up to
\Gl_{\delta_{2}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy, such that
(L^{\prime},\theta^{1}\times\theta^{2})
is conjugated to
(L^{0},\theta^{0})
. In particular, we can take
L_{2}
to be

L_{2}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}(\Gl_{(\beta_{j})_{2}})_{d_{j}}

and
\theta^{2}:L_{2}^{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
the reduced character associated to the
r
-tuple
(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{r})
. We have therefore

\langle m_{\delta},\chi\rangle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{2}}=(\lambda_{1}^{2},\dots,\lambda_{r}^{2})\in\\
\mathcal{P}_{(\beta_{1})_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{P}_{(\beta_{r})_{2}}\end{subarray}}

(6.6.18)

\dfrac{\langle R^{G}_{L_{0}}(\theta^{0}R_{\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{0}}}),R^{G}_{L_{1}\times L_{2}}((\theta^{1}\times\theta^{2})R_{\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{1}}}\boxtimes R_{\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{2}}})\rangle}{q^{-(\delta,\delta)}}\dfrac{(-1)^{(\beta_{1})_{2}+\cdots(\beta_{r})_{2}}}{q^{\frac{\delta_{2}(\delta_{2}-1)}{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}n(\lambda_{j}^{2})}\prod_{j=1}^{r}H_{\lambda_{j}^{2}}(q^{d_{j}})}

(6.6.19)

By Remark
5.6.3
, we have that

\langle R^{G}_{L_{0}}(\theta^{0}R_{\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{0}}}),R^{G}_{L_{1}\times L_{2}}((\theta^{1}\times\theta^{2})R_{\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{1}}}\boxtimes R_{\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{2}}})\rangle=\langle R^{G}_{L_{0}}(\theta^{0}R_{\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{0}}}),R^{G}_{L_{0}}(\theta^{0}R^{L_{0}}_{L_{1}\times L_{2}}(R_{\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{1}}}\boxtimes R_{\overline{\bm{\lambda}_{2}}}))

(6.6.20)

and by Lemma
5.6.1
the RHS of eq.(
6.6.20
) is equal to

\prod_{j=1}^{r}\langle R_{\lambda_{j}^{0}},R^{(\Gl_{(\beta_{j})_{0}})_{d_{j}}}_{(\Gl_{(\beta_{j})_{1}})_{d_{j}}\times(\Gl_{(\beta_{j})_{2}})_{d_{j}}}(R_{\lambda_{j}^{1}}\boxtimes R_{\lambda_{j}^{2}})\rangle_{(\Gl_{(\beta_{j})_{0}})_{d_{j}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}.

By Remark
5.3.2
and Lemma
5.3.3
, for each
j=1,\dots,r
, we deduce that we have an equality

\langle R_{\lambda_{j}^{0}},R^{(\Gl_{(\beta_{j})_{0}})_{d_{j}}}_{(\Gl_{(\beta_{j})_{1}})_{d_{j}}\times(\Gl_{(\beta_{j})_{2}})_{d_{j}}}(R_{\lambda_{j}^{1}}\boxtimes R_{\lambda_{j}^{2}})\rangle_{(\Gl_{(\beta_{j})_{0}})_{d_{j}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}=

(6.6.21)

=\langle\chi_{\lambda_{j}^{0}},\Ind_{S_{(\beta_{j})_{1}}}^{S_{(\beta_{j})_{0}}}\times S_{(\beta_{j})_{2}}(\chi_{\lambda_{j}^{1}}\boxtimes\chi_{\lambda_{j}^{2}})\rangle_{S_{(\beta_{j})_{0}}}=c^{\lambda_{j}^{0}}_{\lambda_{j}^{1},\lambda_{j}^{2}}.

(6.6.22)

From eq.(
6.6.22
), we deduce that we have

\langle m_{\delta},\chi\rangle=\dfrac{1}{q^{-(\delta,\delta)+\frac{\delta_{2}(\delta_{2}-1)}{2}}}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\left(\sum_{\lambda_{j}^{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{(\beta_{j})_{2}}}c^{\lambda_{j}^{0}}_{\lambda_{j}^{1},\lambda_{j}^{2}}\dfrac{(-1)^{(\beta_{j})_{2}}}{q^{-d_{j}n(\lambda_{j}^{2})}H_{\lambda_{j}^{2}}(q^{d_{j}})}\right)

(6.6.23)

From eq.(
6.6.23
) above, we deduce that we have

\dfrac{\langle m_{\delta},\chi\rangle}{\prod_{j=1}^{r}C_{\lambda_{j}^{0},\lambda_{j}^{1}}(q^{d_{j}})}=\dfrac{\prod_{j=1}^{r}q^{d_{j}(|\lambda_{j}^{0}||\lambda_{j}^{1}|-|\lambda_{j}^{0}|^{2}-|\lambda_{j}^{1}|^{2}+\frac{|\lambda_{j}^{2}|^{2}-|\lambda_{j}^{2}|}{2})}}{q^{-(\delta,\delta)+\frac{\delta_{2}(\delta_{2}-1)}{2}}}

(6.6.24)

From the fact that
\delta_{2}^{2}=\delta_{0}^{2}+\delta_{1}^{2}-2\delta_{0}\delta_{1}
and, for each
j=1,\dots,r
,

|\lambda_{j}^{2}|^{2}=|\lambda_{j}^{0}|^{2}+|\lambda_{j}^{1}|^{2}-2|\lambda_{j}^{0}||\lambda_{j}^{1}|

and Identity (
5.9.9
), we deduce that we have

\dfrac{\prod_{j=1}^{r}q^{d_{j}(|\lambda_{j}^{0}||\lambda_{j}^{1}|-|\lambda_{j}^{0}|^{2}-|\lambda_{j}^{1}|^{2}+\frac{|\lambda_{j}^{2}|^{2}-|\lambda_{j}^{2}|}{2})}}{q^{-(\delta,\delta)+\frac{\delta_{2}(\delta_{2}-1)}{2}}}=\dfrac{q^{\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}(-\frac{|\lambda_{j}^{0}|^{2}}{2}-\frac{|\lambda_{j}^{1}|^{2}}{2})}}{q^{-\frac{\delta^{2}_{0}}{2}-\frac{\delta_{1}^{2}}{2}}}=\dfrac{H^{\vee}_{\omega}(q)}{\prod_{j=1}^{r}H^{\vee}_{(1,\bm{\lambda}_{j})}(q^{d_{j}})}.

From the identity above and eq.(
6.6.24
), we deduce therefore equality (
6.6.16
).

∎

We now show how Lemma
6.6.3
implies Theorem
6.5.1
.

Proof of Theorem 6.4.1.

We proceed by induction on the cardinality of
I
.

Let
|I|=1
. The quiver
Q
has thus
1
vertex and
g
loops. Example
6.3.2
shows that in this case, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have an equality
m_{n}=f^{g}_{n}
, where
f^{g}_{n}:\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{C}
is the function defined as

f^{g}_{n}(h)=\dfrac{\#\{(x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{g},y_{g})\in\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{2g}\ |\ \prod_{i=1}^{g}[x_{i},y_{i}]=h\}}{q^{(n^{2}(g-1))}}

introduced in Remark
5.9.6
. It was thereby shown that
\{f^{g}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a dual Log compatible family.

Assume now to have shown Proposition
6.5.1
for all star-shaped quivers with
k
legs and
m
vertices and fix a star-shaped quiver
Q=(I,\Omega)
with
|I|=m+1
. We can assume that
s_{k}>1
.

Let
\tilde{Q}=(\tilde{I},\tilde{\Omega})
be the subquiver of
Q
with set of vertices
\tilde{I}=I-\{[k,s_{k}]\}
and as set of arrows the arrows of
Q
between elements of
\tilde{I}
.

For a dimension vector
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
, we denote by
\tilde{\alpha}
the element of
\mathbb{N}^{\tilde{I}}
obtained by the natural projection
\mathbb{N}^{I}\to\mathbb{N}^{\tilde{I}}
and we denote by
\pi_{\alpha}
the natural projection
\pi_{\alpha}:\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\Gl_{\tilde{\alpha}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
, let
m_{\tilde{\alpha}}
be the function associated to the star-shaped quiver
\tilde{Q}=(\tilde{I},\tilde{\Omega})
and
\tilde{\alpha}
and denote by
n_{\alpha}:\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{C}
the class function defined by

n_{\alpha}(h)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{m_{\tilde{\alpha}}(\pi_{\alpha}(h))}{q^{-\alpha_{[k,s_{k}]}^{2}}}\text{ if }h_{[k,s_{k}]}=1,\\
0\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

From eq.(
5.1.1
), the function
n_{\alpha}
can be rewritten as:

n_{\alpha}(h)=\dfrac{m_{\tilde{\alpha}}(\pi_{\alpha}(h))}{q^{-\alpha_{[k,s_{k}]}^{2}}}\sum_{\mathcal{X}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{[k,s_{k}]}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}}\mathcal{X}(h_{[k,s_{k}]})\dfrac{\mathcal{X}(1)}{|\Gl_{\alpha_{[k,s_{k}]}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}.

(6.6.25)

From Identity (
6.6.25
) and the dual Log compatibility of the functions
\{m_{\tilde{\alpha}}\}
, we can deduce that the family of functions
\{n_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
is dual Log compatible. Indeed, for each
\chi\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, write
\chi=\widetilde{\chi}\boxtimes\chi_{k}
, with
\tilde{\chi}\in\Gl_{\tilde{\alpha}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}
and
\chi_{k}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{[k,s_{k}]}}^{\vee}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. We have

\langle n_{\alpha},\chi\rangle=\dfrac{1}{|\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}\sum_{h\in\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}n_{\alpha}(h)\chi(h)=

(6.6.26)

\left(\sum_{\tilde{h}\in\Gl_{\tilde{\alpha}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\dfrac{m_{\tilde{\alpha}}(\tilde{h})\tilde{\chi}(\tilde{h})}{|\Gl_{\tilde{\alpha}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}\right)\left(\dfrac{q^{\alpha_{[k,s_{k}]}^{2}}}{|\Gl_{\alpha_{[k,s_{k}]}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{X}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{[k,s_{k}]}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}\\
h_{k}\in\Gl_{\alpha_{[k,s_{k}]}}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{\mathcal{X}(1)\mathcal{X}\chi_{k}(h_{k})}{|\Gl_{\alpha_{[k,s_{k}]}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}\right)=

(6.6.27)

=\langle m_{\tilde{\alpha}},\tilde{\chi}\rangle q^{\alpha^{2}_{[k,s_{k}]}}H^{\vee}_{\omega_{\chi_{k}}}(q).

(6.6.28)

and therefore, if we put
\omega_{\chi}=\omega
,
\omega_{\tilde{\chi}}=\tilde{\omega}
and
\omega_{\chi_{k}}=\omega_{k}
, we have

\langle n_{\alpha},\chi\rangle H^{\vee}_{\omega}(q)=\langle m_{\tilde{\alpha}},\tilde{\chi}\rangle H^{\vee}_{\tilde{\omega}}(q)q^{\alpha^{2}_{[k,s_{k}]}}(H^{\vee}_{\omega_{k}}(q))^{2}.

(6.6.29)

Since the family
\{m_{\tilde{\alpha}}\}_{\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{\widetilde{I}}}
is dual Log compatible, from eq.(
6.6.29
), we deduce that Identity (
6.6.25
) is equivalent to show that, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, any
\nu\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
and any
d_{1},\dots,d_{r}
and types
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{r}
such that
\nu=\psi_{d_{1}}(\nu_{1})\ast\cdots\ast\psi_{d_{r}}(\nu_{r})
, we have

\dfrac{(H^{\vee}_{\nu}(t))^{2}}{\prod_{j=1}^{r}(H^{\vee}_{\nu_{j}}(t^{d_{j}}))^{2}}=\dfrac{\prod_{j=1}^{r}t^{d_{j}|\nu_{j}|^{2}}}{t^{n^{2}}}

(6.6.30)

which is a direct consequence of eq.(
5.7.2
).

Let now
\overline{I}=I-\{[k,s_{k}-1],[k,s_{k}]\}
and, for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
, denote by
\overline{\alpha}
the element of
\mathbb{N}^{\overline{I}}
obtained by the natural projection
\mathbb{N}^{I}\to\mathbb{N}^{\overline{I}}
and by
\overline{\pi}_{\alpha}:\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\Gl_{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
the associated projection. For a couple
(\beta,\gamma)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, denote by
m_{(\beta,\gamma)}^{Kr}
the class function associated to the Kronecker quiver and the dimension vector
(\beta,\gamma)
for it, which was studied in Lemma
6.6.3
.

Consider then the function
k_{\alpha}:\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{C}
defined by

k_{\alpha}(h)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{m^{Kr}_{\alpha_{[k,s_{k}-1]},\alpha_{[k,s_{k}]}}(h_{[k,s_{k}-1]},h_{[k,s_{k}]})}{q^{-\sum_{i\in\overline{I}}\alpha_{i}^{2}}}\text{ if }\overline{\pi}_{\alpha}(h)=1\\
0\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}.

As above, from eq.(
5.1.1
), the function
k_{\alpha}
can be rewritten as

k_{\alpha}(h)=\sum_{\chi\in\Gl^{\vee}_{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\chi(\overline{\pi}_{\alpha}(h))\dfrac{\chi(1)}{|\Gl_{\overline{\alpha}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|}\dfrac{m^{Kr}_{\alpha_{[k,s_{k}-1]},\alpha_{[k,s_{k}]}}(h_{[k,s_{k}-1]},h_{[k,s_{k}]})}{q^{-\sum_{i\in\overline{I}}\alpha_{i}^{2}}}.

(6.6.31)

By Identity (
6.6.31
) and Lemma
6.6.3
, similarly to what we did to show eq.(
6.6.25
), we deduce that the family of functions
\{k_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
is dual Log compatible. By Lemma
5.9.5
, the family of class functions

\Biggl\{\dfrac{n_{\alpha}\ast k_{\alpha}}{q^{\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}^{2}}}\Biggr\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}

is dual Log compatible too.

Lastly, a direct calculation shows that, for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
, we have

m_{\alpha}=\dfrac{n_{\alpha}\ast k_{\alpha}}{q^{\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}^{2}}}.

Lemma
5.9.5
implies therefore the family
\{m_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
is dual Log compatible.

∎

6.7
Remarks

Consider any quiver
Q=(I,\Omega)
. As done for a star-shaped quiver in paragraph
section

6.3
, we can define the open subset
R(\overline{Q},\alpha)^{\circ}\subseteq R(\overline{Q},\alpha)
and the multiplicative moment map

\mu_{\alpha}^{\circ}:R(\overline{Q},\alpha)^{\circ}\to\Gl_{\alpha}

(x_{a},x_{a^{*}})_{a\in\Omega}\to\prod_{a\in\Omega}(1+x_{a}x_{a^{*}})(1+x_{a^{*}}x_{a})^{-1}.

For an element
s\in(K^{*})^{I}
, the multiplicative quiver stack
\mathcal{M}_{s,\alpha}
associated to
s
is defined as the quotient stack

\mathcal{M}_{s,\alpha}\coloneqq[(\mu_{\alpha}^{\circ})^{-1}(s)/\Gl_{\alpha}]

Consider the case in which
K=\mathbb{F}_{q}
. We denote by
c_{\alpha}:\Gl_{\alpha}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{C}
the class function given by

c_{\alpha}(g)\coloneqq\dfrac{|(\mu^{\circ}_{\alpha})^{-1}(g)^{F}|}{q^{-(\alpha,\alpha)}}.

We expect that the following result is true:

Conjecture 6.7.1
.

The family of class functions
\{c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
is dual Log compatible.

Similarly to how we proved Theorem
6.5.1
, to prove Conjecture
6.7.1
above, it is enough to show it in the cases where
Q
has a single vertex or
Q
is the Kronecker quiver.

It would be natural to expect that the proofs in these cases are a natural generalization of the proofs of Remark
5.9.6
and Lemma
6.6.3
.

However, in both cases we used in a key way the fact that we considered representations of
\overline{Q}
which were injective along the arrows of
\Omega
. Without the injectivity hypothesis, the combinatorics involved becomes more complicated.

Example 6.7.2
.

Let
Q
be the Jordan quiver and
\alpha=n\in\mathbb{N}
. The function
c_{n}:\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{C}
is given by

c_{n}(g)=\#\{e,e^{*}\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\ |\ (1+ee^{*}),(1+e^{*}e)\in\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\text{ and }(1+ee^{*})(1+e^{*}e)^{-1}=g\}.

Notice that there is no straightforward way to adapt the strategy of proof of Example
5.9.2
to compute the quantity
\langle c_{n},\chi\rangle
for an irreducible character
\chi\in\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{\vee}.

7
Cohomology of character stacks

7.1
Mixed Poincaré series of Artin stacks

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic stack of finite type over an algebraically closed field
K
. Compactly supported cohomology for algebraic stacks have been fully developed in
[
38
]
, where the authors develop a full theory of six-functors formalism for stacks.

In the following, we will always assume that
K=\mathbb{C}
and
\mathcal{X}
is a quotient stack
[X/G]
, where
X
is a
G
-quasi projective variety and
G
is a linear algebraic group.

For such a stack
[X/G]
, compactly supported cohomology can be defined in a simpler way, which is an algebro-geometric version of the Borel construction of equivariant cohomology in differential geometry.

Historically, this was initially taken as a definition of
G
-equivariant compactly supported cohomology or
G
-equivariant Borel-Moore homology of
X
. We briefly review this construction, for more details see
[
17
]
where the authors define in a similar way the equivariant Chow groups or
[
14
, Section 2.5]
.

Consider an embedding
G\subseteq\Gl_{r}(\mathbb{C})
. For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
V_{m}=\Hom(\mathbb{C}^{m},\mathbb{C}^{r})
. Notice that
G
acts on the right on the vector space
V_{m}
and acts freely on the open dense subset
U_{m}=\Hom^{surj}(\mathbb{C}^{m},\mathbb{C}^{r})
, given by surjective homomorphisms.

Consider the left action of
G
on
X\times V_{m}
defined as
g\cdot(x,u)=(g\cdot x,u\cdot g^{-1})
. It is a known fact that the action of
G
on
U_{m}
is schematically free and the quotient stack
[X\times U_{m}/G]
is thus a scheme, which is usually denoted by
X\times_{G}U_{m}
, see for instance
[
17
, Proposition 23]
.

Let
Z_{m}=V_{m}\setminus U_{m}
. The codimension
\codim_{V_{m}}(Z_{m})
goes to
\infty
for
m\to+\infty
. We put then

H^{i}_{c}([X/G],\mathbb{C})\coloneqq H^{i+2\dim(V_{m})}_{c}(X\times_{G}U_{m},\mathbb{C})

for
m
sufficiently big. This definition does not depend on
m
if
m
is sufficiently big, see for example
[
14
, Proposition 2.16]
.

We denote by
H^{*}_{c}([X/G])\coloneqq H^{*}_{c}([X/G],\mathbb{C})
. We can endow these latter cohomology groups with the weight filtration
W^{*}_{\bullet}H^{*}_{c}([X/G])
, defined as follows

W^{i}_{j}H^{i}_{c}([X/G])=W^{i}_{j+\dim(V_{m})}H^{i+2\dim(V_{m})}_{c}(X\times_{G}U_{m})

for
m
sufficiently big. In this case too,
[
14
, Proposition 2.16]
shows that this definition does not depend on
m
if
m
is sufficiently big.

For
\mathcal{X}
over
\mathbb{C}
, we define the mixed-Poincaré series
H_{c}(\mathcal{X},q,t)
as

H_{c}(\mathcal{X};q,t)\coloneqq\sum_{k,m}\dim(W^{k}_{m}/W^{k}_{m-1})q^{\frac{m}{2}}t^{k}.

(7.1.1)

Notice that the specialization
H_{c}(\mathcal{X},1,t)
of
H_{c}(\mathcal{X},q,t)
at
q=1
is equal to the Poincaré series
P_{c}(\mathcal{X},t)
of the stack
\mathcal{X}
. When
\displaystyle\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\dim(W^{k}_{m}/W^{k}_{m-1})
is finite for each
m
, we define the E-series:

E(\mathcal{X},q)\coloneqq H_{c}(\mathcal{X};q,-1)=\sum_{m,k}\dim(W^{k}_{m}/W^{k}_{m-1})(-1)^{k}q^{\frac{m}{2}}.

(7.1.2)

We have the following Theorem, for a proof see
[
43
, Theorem 2.5]
.

Theorem 7.1.1
.

For a quotient stack
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
where
G
is a connected linear algebraic group and
X
an affine variety, the E-series
E(\mathcal{X},q)
is well defined and
E(\mathcal{X},q)=E(X,q)E(BG,q)
where
BG
is the classifying stack of
G
, .

Consider now a complex stack
\mathcal{X}
such that its E-series is well defined. Let now
E
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-finitely generated algebra and
\mathcal{Y}
be an
E
-stack. Assume that there exists
\psi:E\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathcal{Y}\times_{\spec(R),\psi}\spec(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathcal{X}
. The stack
\mathcal{Y}
is called a
spreading out
of
\mathcal{X}
.

For any
\varphi:E\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
, denote by
\mathcal{X}^{\varphi}\coloneqq\mathcal{Y}\times_{\spec(R),\varphi}\spec(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. We say that the stack
\mathcal{X}
is (strongly)
rational count
if there exists an open
U\subseteq\spec(E)
and a rational function
Q(t)
such that for any
\varphi:E\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
with
\varphi(\spec(\mathbb{F}_{q}))\subseteq U
, it holds

\#\mathcal{X}^{\varphi}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}})=Q(q^{n})

for every
n\geq 1
.

In
[
43
, Theorem 2.9]
it is shown the following result.

Theorem 7.1.2
.

For a (strongly) rational count quotient stack
\mathcal{X}
, there is an identity:

E(\mathcal{X},q)=Q(q).

(7.1.3)

Remark 7.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=[X/G]
be a complex quotient stack with
G
a connected linear algebraic group. Let
E
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-subalgebra as above and
Y_{1},Y_{2}
be
E
-schemes which are spreading out of
X,G
respectively.
The stack
\mathcal{Y}\coloneqq[Y_{1}/Y_{2}]
is a spreading out of
\mathcal{X}
.

Notice that for any homomorphism
\varphi:E\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
, there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{X}^{\varphi}=[X^{\varphi}/G^{\varphi}]
and by
[
5
, Lemma 2.5.1]
there is therefore an equality

\#\mathcal{X}^{\varphi}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}})=\dfrac{\#X^{\varphi}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}})}{\#G^{\varphi}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}})}.

(7.1.4)

In particular, the stack
\mathcal{X}
is rational count if and only the variety
X
is polynomial count, i.e. there exists
S(t)\in{\mathbb{Q}}[t]
such that
S(q^{n})=\#X^{\varphi}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}})
for
\varphi
having image in an appropriate open of
\spec(E)
. This was already remarked in
[
43
, Remark 2.7]
.

Consider a reductive algebraic group
G
. In
[
15
]
it is shown that each cohomology group
H^{m}_{c}(BG)
is pure of weight
m
. In
[
15
]
this is stated for cohomology rather than cohomology with compact support. The latter case is an immediate consequence thanks to Poincaré duality.

From Theorem
7.1.2
, we deduce the following Lemma:

Lemma 7.1.4
.

Suppose that
G
is (strongly) polynomial count. The classifying stack
BG
is strongly polynomial count and we have

H_{c}(BG,q,t)=E(BG,qt^{2})=\dfrac{1}{E(G,qt^{2})}.

(7.1.5)

Example 7.1.5
.

We deduce, for instance, that, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

H_{c}(B\Gl_{n},q,t)=\dfrac{1}{(qt^{2})^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}}\dfrac{1}{(qt^{2}-1)\cdots((qt^{2})^{n}-1)}

(7.1.6)

and

H_{c}(B\PGl_{n},q,t)=\dfrac{1}{(qt^{2})^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}}\dfrac{1}{((qt^{2})^{2}-1)\cdots((qt^{2})^{n}-1)}

(7.1.7)

Lastly, we will need the following Proposition about the cohomology of a quotient stack
[X/G]
. Assume that
G=\Gl_{n}
and the center
\mathbb{G}_{m}\subseteq\Gl_{n}
acts trivially on the scheme
X
. There is thus an induced action of
\PGl_{n}
on
X
and a canonical morphism
h:[X/\Gl_{n}]\to[X/\PGl_{n}]
.

Proposition 7.1.6
.

Let
X
be a
\mathbb{C}
-variety with a
\Gl_{n}
-action trivial on the center. We have

H_{c}([X/\Gl_{n}],q,t)=\dfrac{H_{c}([X/\PGl_{n}],q,t)}{qt^{2}-1}

(7.1.8)

Proof.

We start by the case in which
X=\spec(\mathbb{C})
and we look at the canonical morphism
\pi:B\Gl_{n}\to B\PGl_{n}
. In this case, eq.(
7.1.8
) is a direct consequence of eq.(
7.1.6
), eq.(
7.1.7
). There is a cartesian diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\Gl_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\spec(\mathbb{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\PGl_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

where
\psi:\spec(\mathbb{C})\to B\PGl_{n}
is the canonical projection. Since
\psi
is a smooth covering, for each
q\in\mathbb{Z}
, the sheaf
R^{q}\pi_{!}\mathbb{C}
is a local system with fiber
H^{q}_{c}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
.

Moreover, as
\PGl_{n}
is connected, each local system is trivial over
B\PGl_{n}
, see for example
[
2
, Proposition 6.13]
. In particular, the Leray spectral sequence for compactly supported cohomology and the morphism
\pi
in second page is

E^{p,q}_{2}:H^{p}_{c}(B\PGl_{n})\otimes H^{q}_{c}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})\Rightarrow H^{p+q}_{c}(B\Gl_{n}).

From eq.(
7.1.8
), we deduce that the spectral sequence collapses at page
2
, i.e. that the canonical morphism

H^{p}_{c}(B\Gl_{n})\to H^{p}_{c}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})

is surjective for every
p
.

Consider now a general
X
. In this case too, we have a Leray spectral sequence for compactly supported cohomology with second page

E^{p,q}_{2}=H^{p}_{c}\left([X/\PGl_{n}],R^{q}h_{!}\mathbb{C}\right)\Rightarrow H^{p+q}_{c}([X/\Gl_{n}]).

Notice that there is
2
-Cartesian diagram

\textstyle{[X/\Gl_{n}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\Gl_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[X/\PGl_{n}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\PGl_{n}}

where the morphism
\pi:B\Gl_{n}\to B\PGl_{n}
on the right is the canonical morphism between classifying spaces. In particular, we have

R^{q}h_{!}\mathbb{C}\cong r^{*}R^{q}\pi_{!}\mathbb{C}

where we put
r:[X/\PGl_{n}]\to B\PGl_{n}
.

We deduce thus that each
R^{q}h_{!}\mathbb{C}
is trivial. Moreover, the associated map

H^{p}_{c}([X/\Gl_{n}])\to H^{p}_{c}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})

is surjective, since the map
H^{p}_{c}(B\Gl_{n})\to H^{p}_{c}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
is surjective, as remarked above. Therefore, the spectral sequence
E^{p,q}_{2}
collapses at page
2
and we obtain an isomorphism

H_{c}^{*}\left([X/\Gl_{n}]\right)=H_{c}^{*}\left([X/\PGl_{n}]\right)\otimes H^{*}_{c}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}).

∎

7.2
E-series of multiplicative quiver stacks and character stacks

In this paragraph, we apply Theorem
6.5.1
to the computation of E-series of multiplicative quiver stacks and character stacks.

We first recall the results of Hausel, Letellier, Rodriguez-Villegas about the E-series
E(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q)
in the case where
\mathcal{C}
is a
generic

k
-tuple of semisimple conjugacy classes.

7.2.1
Cohomological results for generic
k
-tuples

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
k
-tuple of semisimple conjugacy classes of
\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
. Let
Q=(I,\Omega)
be the associated quiver and
\gamma_{\mathcal{C}}\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{I},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
the associated parameters, introduced in
section

6
.

In
[
31
, Definition 2.1.1]
, the authors give the following definition of a generic
k
-tuple
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 7.2.1
.

A
k
-tuple
\mathcal{C}
of semisimple conjugacy classes is said generic if, given a subspace
W
of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
which is stabilized by some
X_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
, for each
i=1,\dots,k
, such that
\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{k}\det(X_{i}|_{W})=1
then either
W=\{0\}
or
W=\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

Remark 7.2.2
.

For any
\beta\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
, there exists a generic
k
-tuple
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
such that
\alpha_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}=\beta
, see for example
[
31
, Lemma 2.1.2]
.

Lemma 7.2.3
.

If
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}=\{\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}\}
the
k
-tuple
\mathcal{C}
is generic. On the other side, if
\mathcal{C}
is generic, there are no
\delta,\epsilon\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}\setminus\{\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}\}
such that
\delta+\epsilon=\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

Suppose that for a
k
-tuple
\mathcal{C}
there exists a proper subspace
0\subset W\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
X_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,X_{k}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}
such that
X_{i}(W)\subseteq W
for each
i
and

\prod_{i=1}^{k}\det(X_{i}|_{W})=1.

For
i=1\dots,k
and
j=0,\dots,s_{i}
, put
V_{\gamma_{i,j}}=\Ker(X_{i}-\gamma_{i,j}I_{n})
and
W_{\gamma_{i,j}}=W\cap V_{\gamma_{i,j}}
. Notice that, for each
i
, we have

W=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{s_{i}}W_{\gamma_{i,j}}

and

\det(X_{i}|_{W})=\prod_{j=0}^{s_{i}}\gamma_{i,j}^{\dim W_{\gamma_{i,j}}}.

Consider now the dimension vector
\beta\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
defined as

\beta_{[i,j]}=\sum_{h=j}^{s_{i}}\dim(W_{\gamma_{i,j}}).

We have that
\beta<\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}
. Moreover, we have

\gamma_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i,0}^{-\beta_{0}}\prod_{j=1}^{s_{i}}(\gamma_{i,j}^{-1}\gamma_{i,j-1})^{\beta_{[i,j]}}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\gamma_{i,0}^{-\sum_{h=0}^{s_{i}}\dim W_{\gamma_{i,j}}}\prod_{j=1}^{s_{i}}\gamma_{i,j}^{-\sum_{h=j}^{s_{i}}\dim W_{\gamma_{i,j}}}\gamma_{i,j-1}^{\sum_{h=j}^{s_{i}}\dim W_{\gamma_{i,j}}}=

(7.2.1)

=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{j=0}^{s_{i}}\gamma_{i,j}^{-\dim W_{\gamma_{i,j}}}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\det(X_{i}|_{W})^{-1}=1.

(7.2.2)

Conversely, suppose that there exists
\beta\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}\setminus\{\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}\}
such that
\epsilon\coloneqq\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}-\beta
belongs to
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}\setminus\{\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}\}
too.

Since
\epsilon\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
, for each
j,h
, we have

\beta_{[h,j]}-\beta_{[h,j=1]}\leq(\alpha_{\mathcal{C}})_{[h,j]}-(\alpha_{\mathcal{C}})_{[h,j+1]}=m_{h,j},

where
m_{h,j}
is the multiplicity of the eigenvalue
\gamma_{h,j}
in the orbit
\mathcal{C}_{j}
.

Put
m=\beta_{0}
and let
W=\mathbb{C}^{m}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
be the span of the first
m
vectors of the canonical basis. We have that
m<n
, since
\epsilon\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
.

For each
i=1,\dots,k
, there exists a diagonal matrix
X_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
such that its first
m
diagonal entries are given by
\beta_{[i,s_{i}]}
times the element
\gamma_{i,s_{i}}
, then
\beta_{[i,s_{i}-1]}-\beta_{[i,s_{i}]}
times the element
\gamma_{i,s_{i}-1}
and so on.

The subspace
W
is
X_{i}
-stable for each
i=1,\dots,k
and, moreover,

\det(X_{i}|_{W})=\gamma_{\mathcal{C}}^{\beta}=1,

from which we deduce that
\mathcal{C}
is not generic.

∎

For generic
k
-tuples, we have the following general combinatorial formula computing the E-series of the associated character stacks, shown by Hausel, Letellier, Rodriguez-Villegas.

Let
\mathbf{x_{1}}=\{x_{1,1},x_{1,2}\dots\},\dots,\mathbf{x_{k}}=\{x_{k,1},\dots\}
be
k
sets of infinitely many variables and let us denote by
\Lambda_{k}\coloneqq\Lambda(\mathbf{x_{1}},\dots,\mathbf{x_{k}})
the ring of functions over
{\mathbb{Q}}(z,w)
separetely symmetric in each set of variables.

On
\Lambda_{k}
there is a natural
\lambda
-ring structure, induced by the operations
\psi_{d}:\Lambda_{k}\to\Lambda_{k}
defined as

\psi_{d}(f(\mathbf{x_{1}},\dots,\mathbf{x_{k}}))=f(\mathbf{x_{1}}^{d},\dots,\mathbf{x_{k}}^{d})

On
\Lambda_{k}
there is a natural bilinear form obtained by extending by linearity

\left<f_{1}(\mathbf{x_{1}})\cdots f_{k}(\mathbf{x_{k}}),g_{1}(\mathbf{x_{1}})\cdots g_{k}(\mathbf{x_{k}})\right>=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left<f_{i},g_{i}\right>

where
\left<,\right>
is the bilinear form on the ring of symmetric functions making the Schur functions
s_{\mu}
an orthonormal basis. For a multipartition
\bm{\mu}=(\mu^{1},\dots,\mu^{k})\in\mathcal{P}^{k}
we denote by
h_{\bm{\mu}}=h_{\mu^{1}}(\mathbf{x_{1}})\cdots h_{\mu^{k}}(\mathbf{x_{k}})
the associated complete symmetric function.

For any
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}
, let
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(z,w)
be the hook function:

\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(z,w)=\prod_{s\in\lambda}\dfrac{(z^{2a(s)+1}-w^{2l(s)+1})^{2g}}{(z^{2a(s)+2}-w^{2l(s)})(z^{2a(s)}-w^{2l(s)+2})}

(7.2.3)

and the associated series
\Omega(z,w)\in\Lambda_{k}[[T]]

\Omega(z,w)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(z,w)\prod_{i=1}^{k}H_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x_{i}},z^{2},w^{2})T^{|\lambda|}

(7.2.4)

where
H_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x_{i}},q,t)
are the (modified) Macdonald symmetric polynomials (for a definition see
[
25
, I.11]
).

For any
\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}^{k}
, in
[
31
]
it is defined the rational function
\mathbb{H}_{\bm{\mu}}(z,w)
as follows:

\mathbb{H}_{\bm{\mu}}(z,w)\coloneqq(z^{2}-1)(1-w^{2})\left<\Coeff_{T^{n}}(\Plelog(\Omega(z,w))),h_{\bm{\mu}}\right>.

(7.2.5)

For any
\beta\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
and for any
j=1,\dots,k
, the integers
(\beta_{[j,0]}-\beta_{[j,1]},\dots,\beta_{[j,s_{j}-1]}-\beta_{[j,s_{j}]},\beta_{[j,s_{j}]})
up to reordering form a partition
\mu_{\beta}^{j}\in\mathcal{P}
. Denote by
\bm{\mu}_{\beta}\in\mathcal{P}^{k}
the multipartition
\bm{\mu}_{\beta}=(\mu_{\beta}^{1},\dots,\mu_{\beta}^{k})
and by
\mathbb{H}_{\beta}(z,w)
the function
\mathbb{H}_{\bm{\mu}_{\beta}}(z,w)
.

Hausel, Letellier, Rodriguez-Villegas
[
31
, Theorem 5.2.3]
showed the following result:

Theorem 7.2.4
.

For any generic
k
-tuple
\mathcal{C}
, we have:

\dfrac{E(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q)}{q^{-(\alpha_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}})}}=\dfrac{q\mathbb{H}_{\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}\left(\sqrt{q},\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\right)}{q-1}.

(7.2.6)

Remark 7.2.5
.

The result of
[
31
, Theorem 5.2.3]
is stated in a slightly different way. In particular, to verify the equivalence of the results of
[
31
]
and Theorem
7.2.4
, it is needed to verify that
-2(\alpha_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}})+1=\dim(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}})
. The proof of the latter equality can be found at the beginning of
[
32
, Chapter 5.2]
.

In the same paper, the author
[
31
, Conjecture 1.2.1]
proposed the following conjectural identity for the mixed Poincaré series of the character stack
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}
, when
\mathcal{C}
is generic, naturally deforming eq.(
7.2.6
):

Conjecture 7.2.6
.

For any generic
k
-tuple
\mathcal{C}
of semisimple conjugacy classes, we have

\dfrac{H_{c}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q,t)}{(qt^{2})^{-(\alpha_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}})}}=\dfrac{(qt^{2})\mathbb{H}_{\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}\left(-t\sqrt{q},\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\right)}{qt^{2}-1}.

(7.2.7)

Remark 7.2.7
.

Theorem
7.2.4
and Conjecture
7.2.6
in the article
[
31
]
are stated for the corresponding generic character variety
M_{\mathcal{C}}
, rather than the character stack
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}
. The equivalences of the statements of
[
31
]
and those presented here comes from Proposition
7.1.6
.

7.2.2
Main result

Consider a star-shaped
Q=(I,\Omega)
. For any
\sigma\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{I}
and any
\beta\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})
,
we will construct a spreading out of the stack
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\sigma,\beta}
in the following way. Let
E_{0}=\mathbb{Z}[x_{i},x_{i}^{-1}]_{i\in I}
be the ring in
|I|
invertible variables. For any
\delta\in\mathbb{N}^{I}
, denote by
x^{\delta}\in E_{0}
the element
\displaystyle x^{\delta}\coloneqq\prod_{i\in I}x_{i}^{\delta_{i}}
.

Let
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma,\beta}=(\mathbb{N}^{I}_{\leq\beta})^{*}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{\sigma,\beta}.
Consider the multiplicative set
S\subseteq E_{0}
generated by the elements
x^{\delta}-1
for
\delta\in\mathcal{N}_{\sigma,\beta}
.
Denote by
J\subseteq S^{-1}E_{0}
the ideal generated by
(x^{\delta}-1)
for
\delta\in\mathcal{H}_{\sigma,\beta}
and let
E
be the quotient

E\coloneqq S^{-1}E_{0}/J.

Notice that, given a field
K
, a map
\varphi:E\to K
corresponds to an element
\gamma_{\varphi}\in(K^{*})^{I}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{\varphi},\beta}=\mathcal{H}_{\sigma,\beta}
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{0}
be the polynomial
E
-algebra in
\displaystyle 2\sum_{a\in\Omega}s(a)t(a)
variables corresponding to the entries of matrices
(x_{a},x_{a^{*}})_{a\in\Omega}
. Let
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{0}
be the multiplicative system generated by
\det(1+x_{a}x_{a^{*}}),\det(1+x_{a^{*}}x_{a})
for
a\in\Omega
and let
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{W}^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

Consider the ideal
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{0}
generated by the entries of

\prod_{a\in\Omega}(1+x_{a}x_{a^{*}})(1+x_{a^{*}}x_{a})^{-1}-\prod_{i\in I}(x_{i}I_{\alpha_{i}})

and let

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{0}/\mathcal{I}.

Let
Y=\spec(A)
and let
Y^{*}\subseteq Y
be the open subset given by
y\in Y
such that for any algebraically closed field
K
and any morphism
\spec(K)\to Y
with image
y
, corresponding to an element
(x_{a},x_{a^{*}})_{a\in\Omega}\in R(\overline{Q},\alpha,K)
, the maps
(x_{a})_{a\in\Omega}
are injective.

Let now
\psi:E\to\mathbb{C}
be the map induced by the element
\sigma\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{I}
. Notice that

Y^{*}\times_{\spec(E),\psi}\spec(\mathbb{C})\cong(\Phi^{\ast}_{\beta})^{-1}(\sigma)

and therefore
Y^{*}
is a spreading out of
(\Phi^{\ast}_{\beta})^{-1}(\sigma)
. Similarly, for any
\varphi:E\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
corresponding to an element
\gamma_{\varphi}\in(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{*})^{I}
with
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{\varphi},\beta}=\mathcal{H}_{\sigma,\beta}
, we have

((\Phi^{\ast}_{\beta})^{-1}(\sigma))^{\varphi}=(\Phi^{\ast}_{\beta})^{-1}(\gamma_{\varphi}).

Let
\Gl_{\alpha,E}
be the
E
-group scheme
\prod_{i\in I}\Gl_{\alpha_{i},E}
. The stack
\mathcal{Y}^{*}=[Y^{*}/\Gl_{\alpha,E}]
is therefore a spreading out of
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\sigma,\beta}
.

By Remark
7.1.3
and the results of Theorem
5.9.3
and Theorem
6.5.1
, we deduce that the stack
\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\sigma,\beta}
is rational count and we have

\dfrac{E(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\sigma,\beta},q)}{q^{-(\beta,\beta)}}=\Coeff_{\beta}\left(\Plexp\left(\sum_{\delta\in\mathcal{H}_{\sigma}}\widetilde{M}_{\delta,gen}(q)y^{\beta}\right)\right)

(7.2.8)

where
\widetilde{M}_{\delta,gen}(t)
are the rational functions associated to the dual Log compatible family
\{m_{\delta}\}_{\delta\in\mathbb{N}^{I}}
, as in
section

5.9
. Notice that
\widetilde{M}_{\delta,gen}(t)=0
if
\delta\notin(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
.

From Remark
7.2.2
and Lemma
7.2.3
, we deduce that for any
\delta\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
we have

\widetilde{M}_{\delta,gen}(q)=\dfrac{q\mathbb{H}_{\delta}\left(\sqrt{q},\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\right)}{q-1}.

We can resume all the arguments above in the following Theorem:

Theorem 7.2.8
.

For any
\beta\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
and any
\sigma\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{I}
, we have:

\dfrac{E(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{\sigma,\beta},q)}{q^{-(\beta,\beta)}}=\Coeff_{\beta}\left(\Plexp\left(\sum_{\delta\in\mathcal{H}_{\sigma,\beta}^{*}}\dfrac{q\mathbb{H}_{\delta}\left(\sqrt{q},\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\right)}{q-1}y^{\delta}\right)\right)

(7.2.9)

7.2.3
E-series for character stacks with semisimple monodromies

From Theorem
7.2.8
and the isomorphism of Theorem
6.4.2
, we deduce the following Theorem about E-series for character stacks associated to
k
-tuples of semisimple conjugacy classes.

Theorem 7.2.9
.

For any
k
-tuple
\mathcal{C}
of semisimple conjugacy classes of
\Gl_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, we have:

\dfrac{E(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q)}{q^{-(\alpha_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}})}}=\Coeff_{\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}\left(\Plexp\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathcal{H}^{*}_{\gamma_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{q\mathbb{H}_{\beta}\left(\sqrt{q},\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\right)}{q-1}y^{\beta}\right)\right).

(7.2.10)

Remark 7.2.10
.

Notice that Theorem
7.2.10
implies that the E-series
E(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q)
does not depend on the values on the eigenvalues
\{\gamma_{j,h}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1,\dots,k\\
h=0,\dots,s_{j}\end{subarray}}

but only on the subset
\mathcal{H}^{*}_{\gamma_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}
.

8
Mixed Poincaré series of character stacks for
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}
with four punctures

From Theorem
7.2.9
, it seems natural to formulate the following generalization of Conjecture
7.2.6

Conjecture 8.0.1
.

For any
k
-tuple of semisimple conjugacy classes
\mathcal{C}
, we have:

\dfrac{H_{c}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q,-t)}{(qt^{2})^{-(\alpha_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}})}}=\Coeff_{\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}\left(\Plexp\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathcal{H}^{*}_{\gamma_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{(qt^{2})\mathbb{H}_{\beta}\left(t\sqrt{q},\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\right)}{qt^{2}-1}y^{\beta}\right)\right).

(8.0.1)

Remark 8.0.2
.

As mentioned in the introduction, the
-
sign in the term
H_{c}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q,-t)
of eq.(
8.0.1
) is due to the properties of the plethystic exponential
\Plexp
, see for instance
[
12
, Section 4.3]
.

In a nutshell, the plethystic exponential
\Plexp:{\mathbb{Q}}(t)[y_{i}]]^{+}_{i\in I}\to 1+{\mathbb{Q}}(t)[[y_{i}]]^{+}_{i\in I}
can be seen as the decategorification of the symmetric power functor on the category of cohomologically graded and
\mathbb{N}^{I}
-graded vector spaces.

The
-
sign comes then from Koszul’s sign rule for the cohomological grading. We remark that Theorem
7.2.9
is the specialization at
t=1
of Conjecture
8.0.1
.

In this chapter we will verify Conjecture
8.0.1
for a certain family of non-generic character stacks.

Let
\Sigma=\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}
(i.e.
g=0
),
k=4
and
n=2
. Let
D=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{4}\}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}
. For
j=1,\dots,4
, pick
\lambda_{j}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
with
\lambda_{j}\neq\pm 1
and denote by
\mathcal{C}_{j}
the conjugacy class of the diagonal matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{j}&0\\
0&\lambda_{j}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the
k
-tuple
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{4})
. The variety
X_{\mathcal{C}}
is therefore

X_{\mathcal{C}}=\{(X_{1},\dots,X_{4})\in\mathcal{C}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{4}\ |\ X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}X_{4}=1\}.

Denote by
M_{\mathcal{C}}
the GIT quotient
M_{\mathcal{C}}\coloneqq X_{\mathcal{C}}/\!/\Gl_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. Recall that the points of
M_{\mathcal{C}}
are in bijection with the isomorphism classes of semisimple representations of
\pi(\Sigma\setminus D)
inside
X_{\mathcal{C}}
.

The study of the geometry of the character varieties
M_{\mathcal{C}}
goes back to Fricke and Klein
[
21
]
, who gave a description of them in terms of cubic surfaces. Denote by
a_{i}=\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{i}^{-1}
.

The character variety
M_{\mathcal{C}}
is isomorphic to the cubic surface defined by the following equation in
3
variables
x,y,z

xyz+x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}-(a_{1}a_{2}+a_{3}a_{4})x-(a_{2}a_{3}+a_{1}a_{4})y-(a_{1}a_{3}+a_{2}a_{4})z+a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}a_{4}+a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}+a_{3}^{2}+a_{4}^{2}-4=0.

(8.0.2)

If
\mathcal{C}
is generic, this description identifies
M_{\mathcal{C}}
with a smooth (affine) Del Pezzo cubic surface (see
[
20
, Theorem 6.1.4]
), i.e. a smooth cubic projective surface with a triangle cut out of it. The cohomology of this kind of surfaces is well known. In particular, if
\mathcal{C}
is generic, it holds:

H_{c}(M_{\mathcal{C}},q,t)=q^{2}t^{4}+4qt^{2}+t^{2}.

If
\mathcal{C}
is generic, we have that
M_{\mathcal{C}}=[X_{\mathcal{C}}/\PGl_{2}]
. From Proposition
7.1.6
, we have

H_{c}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q,t)=\dfrac{q^{2}t^{4}+4qt^{2}+t^{2}}{qt^{2}-1}.

The identity above agrees with Hausel, Letellier, Rodriguez-Villegas Conjecture
7.2.6
, as explained in
[
31
, Paragraph 1.5]
.

Pick now
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{4}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}\setminus\{1,-1\}
with the following property. For
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{4}\in\{1,-1\}
such that
\lambda_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\cdots\lambda_{4}^{\epsilon_{4}}=1
, then either
\epsilon_{1}=\cdots=\epsilon_{4}=1
or
\epsilon_{1}=\cdots=\epsilon_{4}=-1
. Notice that in this case, the associated
k
-tuple
\mathcal{C}
is not generic.

In the following section, we will compute the mixed Poincaré series
H_{c}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q,t)
and verify that it respects Conjecture
8.0.1
.

For the character stack
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}
, the associated quiver
Q=(I,\Omega)
is the star-shaped quiver with one central vertex and four arrows pointing inwards. We denote the central vertex by
0
and the other vertices by
[i,1]
for
i=1,\dots,4
.

The dimension vector
\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}
is the dimension vector for
Q
defined as
(\alpha_{\mathcal{C}})_{0}=2
and
(\alpha_{\mathcal{C}})_{[i,1]}=1
for
i=1,\dots,4
. The quiver
Q
with the dimension vector
\alpha
is depicted below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

The associated parameter
\gamma_{\mathcal{C}}
is given by

(\gamma_{\mathcal{C}})_{0}=(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}\lambda_{4})^{-1}=1

and, for
i=1,\dots,4

(\gamma_{\mathcal{C}})_{[i,1]}=\lambda_{i}^{2}.

Denote by
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in(\mathbb{N}^{I})^{*}
the elements defined as
(\beta_{1})_{0}=1,(\beta_{1})_{[i,1]}=1
and
(\beta_{2})_{1}=1,(\beta_{2})_{[i,1]}=0
for
i=1,\dots,4
. Notice that it holds
\mathcal{H}^{*}_{\gamma_{\mathcal{C}},\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}=\{\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}
. There are equalities

\mathbb{H}_{\beta_{1}}\left(t\sqrt{q},\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\right)=\mathbb{H}_{\beta_{2}}\left(t\sqrt{q},\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\right)=1.

Conjecture
8.0.1
predicts then the following equality

H_{c}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q,-t)=\dfrac{qt^{2}\mathbb{H}_{\alpha_{\mathcal{C}}}\left(t\sqrt{q},\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\right)}{qt^{2}-1}+\dfrac{q^{2}t^{4}\mathbb{H}_{\beta_{1}}\left(t\sqrt{q},\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\right)\mathbb{H}_{\beta_{2}}\left(t\sqrt{q},\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\right)}{(qt^{2}-1)^{2}}=

(8.0.3)

=\dfrac{q^{2}t^{4}+4qt^{2}+t^{2}}{qt^{2}-1}+\dfrac{q^{2}t^{4}}{(qt^{2}-1)^{2}}=\dfrac{q^{3}t^{6}+4q^{2}t^{4}+qt^{4}-4qt^{2}-t^{2}}{(qt^{2}-1)^{2}}.

(8.0.4)

Since the terms in
t
of the RHS of eq.(
8.0.4
) all have even degrees, in this case Conjecture
8.0.1
is equivalent to verify that

H_{c}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q,t)=\dfrac{q^{3}t^{6}+4q^{2}t^{4}+qt^{4}-4qt^{2}-t^{2}}{(qt^{2}-1)^{2}}.

(8.0.5)

8.1
Cohomology computations

Denote by
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}
the quotient stack
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}=[X_{\mathcal{C}}/\PGl_{2}]
. From Proposition
7.1.6
, we have

H_{c}(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q,t)=\dfrac{H_{c}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}},q,t)}{qt^{2}-1}.

(8.1.1)

We can then reduce ourselves
to compute the cohomology
of the stack
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Inside
X_{\mathcal{C}}
there is the open (dense) subset which we denote by
X_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}\subseteq X_{\mathcal{C}}
, given by quadruple
(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4})\in\mathcal{C}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{4}
corresponding to irreducible representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma\setminus D)
. Recall that
X_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}
is smooth (see for example
[
19
, Proposition 5.2.8]
).

Denote by
\mathcal{N}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}
the quotient stack
[X_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}/\PGl_{2}]
. Notice that the action of
\PGl_{2}
is schematically free on
X_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}

and therefore the stack
\mathcal{N}^{s}_{\mathcal{C}}
is an algebraic variety.

The non irreducible representations of
X_{\mathcal{C}}
all have the same semisimplification, up to isomorphism, which corresponds to the point
m\in M_{\mathcal{C}}
, associated to the isomorphism class of the representation

m=\left(\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{1}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\\
,\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{2}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}^{-1}\\
\end{pmatrix}\\
,\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{3}&0\\
0&\lambda_{3}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\\
,\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{4}&0\\
0&\lambda_{4}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\right).

(8.1.2)

We denote by
O\subseteq X_{\mathcal{C}}
the closed
\Gl_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-orbit associated to
m
. A representation
x\in X_{\mathcal{C}}
which is neither irreducible nor semisimple, i.e. which belongs neither to
X_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}
nor to
O
, can be of the following two types. Either
x
is isomorphic to a quadruple of the form

m^{+}_{a,b,c}\coloneqq\left(\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{1}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\\
,\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{2}&a\\
0&\lambda^{-1}_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\\
,\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{3}&b\\
0&\lambda^{-1}_{3}\end{pmatrix}\\
,\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{4}&c\\
0&\lambda^{-1}_{4}\end{pmatrix}\right)

(8.1.3)

with
(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{(0,0,0)\}
and

\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}c+\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\mu_{4}b+\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}c=0

(8.1.4)

or of the form

m^{-}_{a,b,c}\coloneqq\left(\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda^{-1}_{1}\end{pmatrix}\\
,\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{2}&0\\
a&\lambda^{-1}_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\\
,\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{3}&0\\
b&\lambda^{-1}_{3}\end{pmatrix}\\
,\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{4}&0\\
c&\lambda^{-1}_{4}\end{pmatrix}\right)

with
(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{(0,0,0)\}
and

\lambda_{4}\lambda_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{3}a+\lambda_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{2}^{-1}\lambda_{4}b+\lambda_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{2}^{-1}\lambda_{3}^{-1}c=0.

(8.1.5)

We denote by
Z_{\mathcal{C}}^{+}\subseteq X_{\mathcal{C}}
and by
Z_{\mathcal{C}}^{-}\subseteq X_{\mathcal{C}}

the locally closed subsets of representations isomorphic to elements of the form
m_{(a,b,c)}^{+}
or
m_{(a,b,c)}^{-}

for some
(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\setminus\{(0,0,0)\}
respecting the conditions of eq.(
8.1.4
), eq.(
8.1.5
) respectively.

8.1.1
Cohomology of the character variety in the non-generic case

As mentioned before, the variety
M_{\mathcal{C}}
is a cubic surface defined by equation (
8.0.2
). Denote by
\overline{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}
the associated projective cubic surface. Notice that
\overline{M}_{\mathcal{C}}
is obtained by adding to
M_{\mathcal{C}}
the triangle at infinity
xyz=0
, which we will denote by
U\subseteq\overline{M}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Unlike the case in which
\mathcal{C}
is generic, for our choice of quadruples the surface
\overline{M}_{\mathcal{C}}
is singular, with
m
being is its only singular point. We have moreover an isomorphism
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{C}}^{s}\cong M_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\{m\}
.

It is a well known result (see for example
[
39
]
) that for such a singular cubic surface
\overline{M}_{\mathcal{C}}
, there exists a resolution of singularities

f:\widetilde{M_{\mathcal{C}}}\to\overline{M}_{\mathcal{C}}

such that
f^{-1}(m)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
f
is an isomorphism over
\overline{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\{m\}
, i.e.
\overline{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\{m\}\cong\widetilde{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus f^{-1}(\{m\})
.

Moreover, it is known that
\widetilde{M_{\mathcal{C}}}
is the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}
at
6
points. There is thus an equality

H_{c}(\widetilde{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q,t)=q^{2}t^{4}+7qt^{2}+1.

Using the long exact sequence in compactly supported cohomology for the open-closed decomposition
\widetilde{M}_{\mathcal{C}}=f^{-1}(\overline{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\{m\})\bigsqcup f^{-1}(m)
, we deduce that we have

H_{c}(\overline{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\{m\},q,t)=H_{c}(f^{-1}(\overline{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\{m\}),q,t)=q^{2}t^{4}+6qt^{2}

and so that we have

H_{c}(\overline{M}_{\mathcal{C}},q,t)=q^{2}t^{4}+6qt^{2}+1.

It is not difficult to check that the compactly supported Poincaré polynomial of
U
is
H_{c}(U,q,t)=3qt^{2}+t+1
